### LADO B  ·  screen 6.2/10

**REGISTER** sound / food-and-hand-off / people  
**ARTEFACT** A single-sided 7-inch 45 rpm vinyl single, black, in a 178 mm square uncoated sleeve die-cut with the serving aperture at exactly 1:3. Labels one ink. First run 300 copies, sold across the counter.

> Side A is one unbroken take of food going through the hole; side B is blank, because the room this bus was cut open in was never recorded and cannot be.

SIDE A -- SERVICIO. One unbroken take of the service window: the order called, the paper, the tray crossing the brass line, the plancha under all of it, plates and coats and somebody's kid. No edit, no fade, no music, no bed. The microphone stands at the geometric centre of the middle serving aperture, 1508.5 mm above ground, facing out. No human height exists anywhere in this record, so no human height is used -- the ear is the hole. Run time is a manufacturing figure, not an artistic one: roughly four and a half minutes is what a 45 rpm seven-inch side holds at full level, a trade rule of thumb, not a measurement of anything here. The final groove is LOCKED: 1.3333 seconds of griddle at 45 rpm, exactly, repeating until somebody lifts the arm.

SIDE B -- PLAYA DEL CARMEN, 2006. NO SE GRABO. Blank, unmodulated lacquer. Play it and you hear the record, not the room, because the room where a man figured out how to cut open the Tacombi was never recorded and cannot be. One line on the label, his project's own sentence: IT CANNOT BE RECOVERED FROM WHAT WE HOLD.

The sleeve is the serving aperture at exactly 1:3 -- 171.8 x 134.3 mm, corner radius 18.3 -- die-cut clean out of the 178 mm square, leaving 3.1 mm of card each side. You see the label through the hole. That is how you see everything else on this vehicle.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — SMALL, AND I WILL NOT INFLATE IT. The model contributes exactly two things: the microphone's height, 1508.5 mm above ground, derived rather than guessed; and the sleeve die, which is the aperture at a stated 1:3 with its measured 55 mm corner radius, so the hole in the card is the hole in the bus divided by three. Everything else here is a person, a recorder and a Tuesday. That is a feature. This is the one artefact in the set that would exist if the 3D model had never been built, and after 76 revisions and forty of them spent making a bus that only ever floats on white paper, shipping something that needs a microphone and not a render is the point rather than an admission.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — FIVE. (1) CONSENT. Publishing customers' and staff voices on a commercial record is not the same as recording in your own room; identifiable voices need releases and the counter needs a sign on the day. The sign is hand-lettered in the house idiom -- HOY GRABAMOS -- and it is a cost, not a decoration. (2) THE BLANK SIDE READS AS A RIP-OFF. Half a record at a full price is a legitimate customer complaint and it will be made out loud. It is priced as half a record and the reason is printed on the sleeve. (3) IT IS ONE STEP FROM A CAGE JOKE and some people will only ever hear the joke. (4) F92. The Playa hero is RULED 'DEPRIORITISED, NOT CANCELLED' and the carriers say do not act on it either. THIS DOES NOT BUILD THE PLAYA HERO -- it prints that Playa is unrecordable, which is the opposite move. Rule 34's object test: the ruling's object is a RENDER; this is a blank groove. If he reads it as working that deliverable anyway, it is his call and it dies. (5) A real service is loud, ugly and boring for four minutes at a stretch. There is no guarantee the take is any good, and one take is the whole idea.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Aperture centre 1508.5 mm above ground -- DERIVED from Z_SILL 1372.0, Z_HEAD 1775.0 and the locked RIDE_DROP 65.0, computed and watched print this session. Serving aperture 515.5 x 403.0 mm, corner radius 55, three, EQUAL -> sleeve art at 1:3 = 171.83 x 134.33 mm, r 18.3, inside a 178 mm square with 3.08 mm of card each side. Locked groove at 45 rpm = 1.3333 s exactly (60/45, watched print). Label ink is INK (72,46,6), and the label itself carries the flag CONSTRUCTED -- the measured median is (91,59,7) and is blur-limited, which is why the core was set darker. UNMEASURED, and it is the entire point of side B: everything about Playa del Carmen in 2006.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Recording day: free on a decent handheld recorder, or order of 500-900 USD for an engineer (ESTIMATE). A single-sided 7-inch pressing at 300 copies, lacquer and plates included, is order of 900-1,500 USD -- roughly 3-5 USD a unit (ESTIMATE). A 25-copy lathe-cut trial for the owner and staff is order of 12-18 USD each (ESTIMATE) and needs no plates at all. The sleeve die is order of 150-300 USD once, plus one-ink print on uncoated board. First run lands under about 2,500 USD all in. Everything except the die is repeatable per location: Nolita now, and every room the brand opens later gets its own side A.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — THIS WEEK. Tape a recorder at the middle aperture -- 1508.5 mm, measure it with a tape, do not eyeball it -- and record one thirty-minute service. Cut nothing. Send him four and a half minutes of it with a flat printed mock-up of the die-cut sleeve at 1:3 that he can hold up to the light and look through. Two objects, one afternoon, under 50 USD: a sound he has heard ten thousand times and never once listened to, and a piece of card with a hole in it that is his own serving window divided by three.

### CAJA DE RESONANCIA  ·  screen 4.83/10

**REGISTER** sound  
**ARTEFACT** Six 24-bit / 48 kHz stereo impulse responses released free as WAV under CC-BY; a 12-page A5 booklet, ONE ink on warm uncoated stock, 500 copies; and one deployed 60-second seamless telephone hold loop at G.711 mu-law, 8 kHz mono, 300-3400 Hz.

> Tacombi's sonic identity is not a jingle, it is a room -- every sound the brand emits arrives having been played inside a 1963 combi.

Everything the brand emits -- phone hold, every video, the in-store bed -- is convolved with the bus's own impulse response. Not a mark. A room.

Six positions, each taken off the mesh rather than chosen by ear. AT THE WINDOW: receiver at the middle serving aperture's geometric centre, 1508.5 mm above ground, derived from Z_SILL 1372.0 and Z_HEAD 1775.0 less the locked RIDE_DROP 65.0. ACROSS THE COUNTER, from outboard of a slab measured 107.0 mm thick and 321.0 mm deep. UNDER THE LID at its declared 76.0 degrees. TRES HUECOS with all three apertures open -- 0.61545 m2 of hole. INSIDE. And MOTOR: NINGUNO, the empty engine bay: the most reverberant space on this vehicle is the one with nothing in it.

The booklet's first page is the ceiling, printed before any result: GEOMETRY MEASURED, ABSORPTION DECLARED. The shape of this room is the most accurately known thing about it; how its steel and its cream paint soak up sound is a coefficient table nobody has ever checked against this vehicle.

Then the correction that makes it real. Record the actual impulse response in the Nolita room -- a balloon, then a sweep -- and score the simulation against it. If the model predicts the room, publish both and say by how much. If it does not, publish that: a solver that cannot predict a steel box you can walk into is worth more than the pack.

Release the files free, credited to the vehicle. Producers will use them, and the credit line carries the bus into records nobody paid for.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Decisive, and for a reason nothing else in the programme uses. A geometric-acoustics solver has to decide inside from outside: the body has ZERO non-manifold edges and 229,489 quads against 1,040 tris, which is exactly why this mesh will close and a photogrammetry scan of the real bus would not. 229 named mesh objects and 45 material datablocks let absorption be assigned per surface instead of per room. Aperture area, aperture centre height and the counter's plan depth are measured, so the six listening positions are stated in millimetres rather than picked by ear. What the model does NOT give is absorption -- that is declared off a published table, and page 1 of the booklet says so before any number appears. BLOCKER, NAMED: there is no 3D export path in this tree today, only a .blend save. A mesh writer is the gate on the simulation half; the recorded half needs no model at all.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — FOUR, AND THE FIRST IS THE OWNER'S OWN SHAPE. (1) F314: 'I can't tell them apart -- stop tuning the weight.' Convolution reverb at sane mix levels is exactly the kind of sub-visible tuning he has already ended once. If he cannot hear the bus, this dies, and it should. (2) IT MAY SIMPLY SOUND BAD. A small steel box has flutter echo between parallel flanks and a boxy 200-500 Hz honk. 'Everything we release sounds like a tin can' is a plausible outcome, not a scare story, and the honk gets shipped audible or not at all -- burying it at 5 % wet is how this becomes a lie about itself. (3) THE DELIVERY MEDIUM THROWS AWAY THE EXPENSIVE PART. The telephone band starts at 300 Hz. The whole low end of the box -- the part a measured cavity is uniquely good for -- is gone before the caller hears it, and that is printed in the booklet rather than hidden. (4) A SIMULATED IR PRESENTED AS A MEASURED ONE IS A FRAUD IN THE AUDIO WORLD. Every file name carries SIM or REC and the two are never blended.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Serving aperture 515.5 x 403.0 mm, corner radius 55, THREE, EQUAL (BAY_W = 0.5155 'equal, measured'; Z_SILL 1.3720 / Z_HEAD 1.7750, read from t1_shell.py this session). Open area computed this session with corner radii subtracted and watched print: 0.20515 m2 per aperture, 0.61545 m2 for three. Aperture centre 1508.5 mm above ground -- derived ((1372.0+1775.0)/2 minus RIDE_DROP 65.0), watched print. Counter slab 107.0 mm thick, 321.0 mm plan depth, top 1254 mm. Roof lid opening 76.0 deg DECLARED. Rear hatch 64.0 deg DECLARED -- no frame shows it. Body 4065 x 1750 mm. Non-manifold edges (body) 0; 229 mesh objects; 229,489 quad / 1,040 tri / 5,186 ngon; 45 material datablocks. Plancha 560 x 780 mm (t1_detail.py, read this session). UNMEASURED and printed as such: every acoustic absorption coefficient, and the real vehicle's reverberation time.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — The recorded half is nearly free: a balloon and a phone, one afternoon. Doing it properly -- omni mic, powered speaker, exponential sine sweep, deconvolution -- is order of 500-900 USD for a day-rate engineer or 600-1500 USD of gear (ESTIMATES, not measurements). The simulated half runs on open-source geometric acoustics; compute is trivial next to a 5-minute Cycles frame, but it needs the mesh export that does not exist yet -- call it a contained code job, not a research project. Booklet: A5, 12pp, one ink, uncoated, 500 copies, order of 400-800 USD (ESTIMATE). Hold-loop deployment costs whatever the phone provider charges to upload a WAV, which is usually nothing. Total to a shippable v1 is under about 2,000 USD and most of that is paper.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — THIS WEEK, FOR ZERO DOLLARS. Walk into the Nolita room, stand inside the bus, and pop a balloon -- once with the serving apertures open, once with them shut. Two 6-second files on his phone and one sentence: 'this is what the inside of your bus sounds like, and in twenty years nobody has ever recorded it.' He can play it at the table. If he shrugs, the concept is dead on Tuesday for the cost of a balloon, which is the cheapest kill this programme has ever been offered.

## PACKAGING AS A SYSTEM

### ARMA TU COMBI  ·  screen 7.13/10

**REGISTER** child, and polychrome — eight measured inks against a programme that had been running on two  
**ARTEFACT** Eight kiss-cut vinyl seals, one per package, each on a 60 × 60 mm backing that is itself the tamper seal. White gloss vinyl, digital CMYK + white, kiss-cut. Peeled and assembled on any flat surface they make one 1:24 combi, 169.375 mm long. THE EIGHT: front wheel Ø27.704 (salsa cup) · rear wheel (second salsa cup) · the flank, 3 apertures at 21.479 × 16.792 r 2.292, cut THROUGH so the surface behind becomes the galley (taco box) · the counter, 129.417 mm with a 0.829 mm brass line and its measured clutter (tray) · the roof lid with the mural, 84.750 mm (bag) · the tail board, chord 29.625 mm (carrier) · the festoon, a 139.033 mm strip of 118 lamps at 1.188 mm pitch, printed LIT (napkin band) · and the nose, HELD.

> Every package closes with one die-cut part of the bus, at true 1:24, and eight packages build the whole thing on a child's fridge — except the nose, which we are not allowed to issue yet.

This is F18. The die-cut sticker is the register's oldest live row, open since rev 44, the original deliverable, the only artefact with a style the owner locked himself — 'cartoon with rendered depth, vector line and flat colour, shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset' — a named audience, children at the restaurant, and a written bar: spark joy, be something families keep. A takeaway bag is how a sticker gets home.

Which seal you get is decided by what you ordered, not by a cook making a choice during service. Salsa gives you a wheel. Tacos give you the flank. The bag gives you the roof lid, mural and all: ten full flower heads and one half head cut by the right strip, eight petals each, in the five inks measured off that board — 55/26/19, 159/45/22, 163/66/21, 172/119/24, 184/150/76 — plus the body red, the cream and the strip yellow. Eight measured colours.

The flank's three apertures are cut clean through the vinyl, so whatever it is stuck to becomes the galley. On the box, the food shows through. On a fridge door, the fridge does.

The festoon strip is the one part that does something the photoreal render cannot: 118 lamps, printed lit and warm, where the render puts them out as unlit pearl white while both in-service photographs show them burning.

And the nose is not issued. The roundel is 11.667 mm at this scale and it is not right — 0.8528 pose-free IoU against a control ceiling of 0.9465, rejected nine times. The eighth seal ships the day he says the emblem is right.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Total, and this is the concrete argument for the keystone. All eight parts must be cut from ONE true orthographic side elevation or they will not register when a child assembles them — and the side ortho camera fits today (5.90 → 4.0563 m against a 4.065 m body), while front and rear clip. The 229 named meshes give per-part masks with true relative positions, which is what makes 'the counter' and 'the flank' separable objects rather than crops. And the craft link is exact: a kiss-cut vinyl, a die-cut sticker and a Huixcolotla fierrito struck through a stack of tissue are the same operation — a closed cut path — which is precisely what Grease Pencil Line Art → SVG emits. The locked style also needs the AO pass. Build the pass, and F18 falls out of it.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — It is a loyalty mechanic wearing a toy's clothes and it is aimed at children. The set only completes if a family buys across the menu; a child who only ever gets salsa gets two wheels and no bus. I am not softening that — it is the actual mechanism and the owner should decide with his eyes open. Second, and worse: the eighth seal is withheld pending an internal engineering grievance no child can see, so if the emblem is never right the bus is never finished and the project's oldest failure becomes a promise broken to a customer at a counter. Third: kiss-cut vinyl is plastic stuck to a paper package and it kills any recyclability claim the rest of the system makes. Fourth, a declared departure, not a hidden one — his LOCKED SCENE for F18 is 'nothing but the bus, die-cut tight'. Assembled, this is exactly that. Issued, it is eight parts, and that is his ruling to make, not mine.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Scale set from body length 4065 mm → 169.375 mm at 1:24 (all following watched print): counter 3106.0 → 129.417; festoon rail 3336.8 → 139.033; tyre Ø664.9 → 27.704; hubcap Ø274.0 → 11.417; cap badge Ø86.9 → 3.621; VW roundel Ø280.0 → 11.667; roof lid 2034 → 84.750; aperture 515.5 × 403.0 r 55.0 → 21.479 × 16.792 r 2.292, three and EQUAL; tail chord 711.0 → 29.625; brass band 19.90 → 0.829; lamp Ø22.0 → 0.917 at 1.188 pitch. Festoon: BULB_X0 −1.6368, BULB_X1 1.7000, pitch 0.0286 → 117 segments, 118 lamps, realised 28.520 mm. Mural inks 55/26/19, 159/45/22, 163/66/21, 172/119/24, 184/150/76; body RED 196/49/36; CREAM 206/208/200; strip yellow 172/144/17. Mural structure: 10 full heads + 1 half, 8 petals (k=8 power 42.6 against k=12 at 6.3). Emblem 0.8528 against ceiling 0.9465.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Manufacturing is the cheap half: no plate cost, digital print plus kiss-cut, eight SKUs of one 60 × 60 backing, roughly £0.06–0.12 a seal in short run (ESTIMATE, not a measured figure). The expensive half is drawing eight parts in a style he locked and we have never built: the Line Art → SVG pass and an AO pass do not exist in this tree. DECLARATIONS. SPEC §10.10 / F94, artwork replication as a hard bar: this REPRODUCES the artwork, it does not deface it — but at 84.750 mm the mural's petals are about a millimetre each, so it is drawn at its measured STRUCTURE and not at its resolution, and the backing card says so. The sun and the papel picado are NOT drawn: the record refutes both on evidence, and he deferred the papel picado to himself. Do not re-put it.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — The festoon seal, this week, and it needs no new render pass at all — it is a rail, 118 circles and a wire, drawable tonight straight off BULB_X0 −1.6368, BULB_X1 1.7000, BULB_PITCH 0.0286. Print it 139.033 × 12 mm, lamps lit and warm, kiss-cut on a backing, stick it along the top of a plain kraft bag and put it in his hand. Then ask the one question this programme has never asked, the one that unblocks the oldest row on the register: he deferred the sticker with 'build it after the model is done' — is a takeaway bag the trigger? Offer it as multiple choice with the bag attached. Note for honesty on the face of it: the built count is 118 lamps at a realised 28.520 mm pitch; the 115 in circulation is an FFT inference about a 3.50 m rail, not this model, and it should stop being quoted.

**REGISTER** food-and-hand-off, and people — two-sided printing divided by the counter's own brass line  
**ARTEFACT** A four-piece two-sided family. LA CHAROLA: one-piece die-cut board tray, 321.0 mm front-to-back — the counter's whole plan depth — × 215 mm, 25 mm walls, 2 colours plus a single 19.9 mm metallic band. LA SERVILLETA: 1-ply napkin, 200 mm square, one ink, coarse halftone. EL VASO: 12 oz paper cup, one ink. EL RECIBO: 80 mm thermal roll. Every piece has a COOK FACE and a GUEST FACE, and the only place they meet is the gold band.

> The tray is exactly as deep as the counter, and the 19.9 mm gold band across it marks the instant the food stops being the restaurant's and becomes yours.

The counter slab is 107.0 mm thick, 321.0 mm deep in plan, its top 1254.0 mm above ground, and its outer edge is capped in brass over 0.186 of that edge — 19.9 mm of gold, measured at 4.52 ± 0.23 px on a 24.84 px edge and corroborated at 0.191 in a second frame, so 19.5–20.4 mm honestly stated.

The tray is 321.0 mm deep. It arrives spanning the whole slab. As the cook pushes it, one moment its leading edge crosses the brass, and a 19.9 mm gold band is printed across the tray at exactly that crossing. That band is the entire ceremony of this business.

The blank's inner face becomes the tray's underside, so the cook loads onto a face nobody will see until the meal is over. On it: the galley section — plancha 560 × 780, top at 1455 mm; the warm strip light tucked under the head rail; six hooks; two hanging tools — with the depth axis stamped DEPTH NOT MEASURABLE, because Y was chosen everywhere and never measured. You lift the empty tray and get the cook's side of the counter.

The napkin carries the five people the survey discarded: the eater in the red shirt whose shirt every attempt to measure the front arch locked onto; the woman with her chin on her hand, waiting; the cook in the white cap; the woman standing in the freshly cut roof with both hands on the lid; the child in the magenta coat, three of whose hairs cost residuals of −16.7, −16.1 and −13.6 px. Each at the halftone the measurement actually saw them at, residual set 5 pt beside.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Partial and specific. The tray's one important dimension — 321.0 mm — is a plan depth off the mesh (CNT_Y_IN 0.8450 → CNT_Y_OUT 1.1660), and the band is CNT_NOSE_F 0.186 × the 107.0 mm slab edge, which prints in the source as '19.9 mm of gold in elevation'. The underside's galley section needs the built fit-out's true X and Z. The napkin needs no model at all — those are photographs, and I say so rather than fake a dependency. F92 DECLARATION: this is not the Playa hero. F92 is RULED-rev57b 'DEPRIORITISED, NOT CANCELLED', its object is a RENDER, and a printed napkin is a different object under rule 34's own test. But its sentence is the bar this concept aims at: 'the emotional bar that sits ABOVE clinical accuracy.'

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Seven identifiable people, no releases, and the eater is frontal and recognisable. A coarse halftone is not consent and I will not pretend it is — either they are cleared or the napkin ships with four people instead of five and we say which one is missing and why. Second: printing a customer's face on a napkin the next customer wipes their hands on is either the most moving object in this whole programme or the most tasteless, and I genuinely cannot tell which from here; the owner can, in three seconds, which is why the first step is one napkin. Third, operationally: a 321 mm tray is large for a taco counter, will not stack more than about four high loaded, and eats counter space at the exact moment the counter has none. Fourth: the gold band is a third print unit for 19.9 mm of ink, and the first accountant who sees it will kill it.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Counter: CNT_ZT 1.2540 / CNT_ZB 1.1470 = 107.0 mm thick; CNT_Y_IN 0.8450 → CNT_Y_OUT 1.1660 = 321.0 mm plan depth; CNT_X0 0.9180 → CNT_X1 −2.1880 = 3106.0 mm long (watched print); CNT_NOSE_F 0.1860 × 107.0 = 19.90 mm brass, spread 19.47–20.44 mm across the two frames. Galley: plancha 560 × 780, top 1455 mm AG; strip-light emission (1.000, 0.918, 0.790) at GAL_LUM 3.04; galley Y NOT MEASURABLE. Probe residuals −16.7 / −16.1 / −13.6 px on a child's hair; 37 % of bay-1 variance is the vendor. Brass linear (0.6600, 0.4750, 0.1750), roughness 0.255.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Tray: one dieline, 350 gsm, 2 colours plus a gold unit or foil pass — the band is the expensive 19.9 mm in the system (ESTIMATE, not measured). Napkin: 1-colour flexo on 1-ply at a coarse screen around 34 lpi; the low screen is the concept and it is the cheapest thing on the sheet. Cup and receipt are stock items with a plate change. No new render passes needed for the napkin; the tray underside needs one section drawing off the built galley, which is a day. Note in passing, so it is not lost: the record carries 267 ELIZABETH STREET off the photograph against SPEC's 251, and until he resolves it this family prints no address at all.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — One napkin, this week. 200 mm square, one ink, the woman with her chin on her hand at a 34 lpi screen, and under her in 5 pt: '37 % of the variance is the man, and no dressing can produce it.' A laser proof on napkin stock costs nothing. Hand it to him with exactly one question: does this belong on a napkin, or on a wall? He answers a bigger question than the one asked — he always does — and whichever way he goes we learn whether this programme is allowed to have people in it at all.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:packaging-2`

### SE ABRE  ·  screen 4.83/10

**REGISTER** three-dimensional — structural packaging, where the payload is the fold and the print is subordinate  
**ARTEFACT** Five die-cut folding pieces in 350 gsm unbleached kraft, ONE ink (body RED, sRGB 196/49/36), grease barrier on the two food-contact pieces. LA CAJA, taco box, footprint 257.75 × 201.50 mm r 27.50 (the serving aperture at 1:2), lid hinge 76.0°. LA BOLSA, bag, gusset carrying TWO score lines at 38.0° and 28.0°. EL PORTAVASOS, four-cup carrier, top flap 64.0°. EL SALSERO, salsa cup, 0.0° — sealed, tears open, never hinges. LA TARJETA, the table card, 82.0°. Each piece prints, in the same single ink at its own hinge, a quadrant arc with its angle and its provenance grade.

> Five packages, five opening angles, each one lifted from a specific opening on the bus and printed with how badly we know it.

This bus is a thing that opens. Five openings, five constants, and every one of them carries a different quality of truth: the roof lid at 76.0° DECLARED inside brackets that intersect at 68.9–78°; the rear hatch at 64.0°, guarded in source to keep saying NOT MEASURED because no frame shows it; the tail board BUILT at 38.0° and PHOTOGRAPHED at 28.0°, two rulers, unresolved since rev 62; the lower bay at 0.0° because the owner ruled it shut; and 82.0°, the propped signboard he told us is not part of the bus at all. So the one piece that stays on the table is the one that is furniture.

The bag is the best object here. It has two creases. You can fold it at 38.0° or at 28.0° and it works either way, because nothing in this record resolves which is right. Seven revisions of disagreement, in your hand, in kraft.

Each piece prints its own protractor at the hinge. Open it, lay it flat against the arc, and look. Board springs back; the arc will show the miss. That is not a defect to engineer out — it is the first thing this project has ever printed that you can watch fail in public.

No elevation, no roundel, no artwork. This system composes the emblem out entirely, and it does so on purpose, so F191 has nothing to gate.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Total. A hinge angle is not in any photograph — it is a pose constant with provenance attached, and only the mesh has them: LID_OPEN_DEG 76.0, REAR_OPEN_DEG 64.0 (guarded NOT MEASURED), TB_TILT_DEG 38.0, TRUNK_OPEN_DEG 0.0 (owner ruling rev 49), SIGN_OPEN_DEG 82.0. The box footprint is the serving aperture halved — 515.5 × 403.0 r 55, ray-tested through the shell, three and equal. Photogrammetry gives you none of this; a photograph gives you one angle, badly.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — It is an in-joke about an internal disagreement, printed on a stranger's lunch. Four of the five angles are DECLARED or contested and only 38.0/28.0 has any photographic support at all — so a customer holding this is holding a set of guesses dressed as a spec. Worse for us: the printed arc guarantees public failure. Kraft board at 350 gsm springs back several degrees off any score, so a lid intended at 76.0° will sit visibly off its own printed arc, every time, on every box. I am not designing that out. If the owner wants a package that keeps its promises, this is the wrong package. Also, the register is austere and dry: this is the concept a design-literate adult loves and a child ignores completely.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — LID_OPEN_DEG 76.0 (DECLARED, brackets 68.9–78); REAR_OPEN_DEG 64.0 (NOT MEASURED, no frame shows it); TB_TILT_DEG 38.0 built vs 28.0 photographed (different rulers, unresolved); TRUNK_OPEN_DEG 0.0 (owner ruling rev 49); SIGN_OPEN_DEG 82.0 (separate signboard, owner: 'The forward one is separate from the Bus'); serving aperture 515.5 × 403.0 mm r 55.0, three, EQUAL — halved to 257.75 × 201.50 r 27.50 (watched print); body RED sRGB 196/49/36.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Cheapest family of the three to manufacture and the slowest to draw. Five cutting formes at roughly £120–£200 each (ESTIMATE, not a measured figure); one-colour flexo or litho on kraft; no foil, no emboss, no varnish beyond the barrier. Short run 1,000 of each. The real cost is structural prototyping: five dielines each needing three or four physical mock-ups to get the score geometry right, which is a week of a packaging engineer, not of a renderer. No new render passes required — the angles are already constants.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — The bag, this week, cut and scored by hand from a single sheet of kraft: one blank, two creases, one printed arc. Fold it once at 38.0° and once at 28.0°, put both beside the IMG_3840.jpeg crop, and ask him one question with the question tool — which fold matches the photograph? He has never stood in the bus, so the question is about the photograph, which is the only form he can answer. CEILING, stated to him in the same sentence: that frame is 480 × 320 with the board spanning ~90 px, two significant figures, and the two figures were taken with different rulers. The bag cannot resolve it. It can only make him look at it, which nothing in seven revisions has managed.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — False  ·  **id** `dream:packaging-1`

## BEAUTIFUL RATHER THAN RIGOROUS

### PROVECHO  ·  screen 8.73/10

**REGISTER** food-and-hand-off — a painted image, warm and unashamed (territory: beautiful-not-rigorous)  
**ARTEFACT** A lithographed tin serving tray. 380 × 290 mm, rolled edge, six colours plus clear varnish, offset-litho on 0.28 mm tinplate. And a second size, 240 × 180 mm — the charolita, the child's tray. One painted image, one word.

> A tin tray carrying the one picture this project has never made — two pairs of hands crossing the counter with a plate — and one word: PROVECHO.

A tin tray, litho-printed, the kind that outlives three refits.

One painting on it, and it is the image seventy-six revisions have never made: the moment the food crosses. You are standing at the counter. Out of the hatch come the cook's hands with a plate; yours are going up to meet them at the bottom of the frame, close enough that you can see they are yours. Behind him the strip light lies in a soft band down the galley wall, the white going warm-cream at the seam where the material changes. The bobble fringe runs round the aperture, ball after ball after ball. Along the counter's lip, a thin bright strip of brass. On the plate, four tacos and a wedge of lime.

Painted — gouache, or something that behaves like gouache — not rendered. Warm, generous, a little over-saturated, the way a good tin tray from 1965 is over-saturated. Round the rim, in a signwriter's brush script, one word: PROVECHO. That is the only word on the object.

Seventy-six revisions have been spent on a bus with nobody in it. This is a taqueria. The transaction is a person handing you hot food through a hole, and until that is on an object we have made an extremely accurate picture of an empty vehicle. A tray is the cheapest way to put it in his hand — he can carry one out to a table the day it arrives.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The armature only, and then it is painted over until nothing of it shows. What the model gives is the one thing an illustrator cannot guess: the true perspective from a standing adult's eye at a counter whose top is 1254 mm above ground, looking into a hole 515.5 mm wide with its head at 1710 mm and its sill at 1307 mm. Get that and the hands come out the right size, the fringe balls come out at the right density instead of being decoration, and the brass line lands where it actually lands. All nine reference photographs are shot from further back and not one of them is taken AT the pass. The model is the only way to stand where the customer stands. It contributes a wireframe and then disappears.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — (1) EVERYTHING WARM IN THIS IMAGE IS UNMEASURED. There is no human geometry anywhere in this project — no hand, no figure, no proxy, no scale mannequin — and no food geometry either: the plancha is a bare 0.560 × 0.780 slab that has never had anything on it. The hands, the forearms, the plate, the tacos, the lime are all invented, and the owner is a restaurateur who has watched ten thousand of these hand-offs. If the taco is wrong he will see it in half a second and he will be right. The mitigation is not measurement, it is showing him the drawing early and letting him correct the food. (2) LIKENESS. The cook in `ref_side.jpg` is a real employee in a white cap and an apron carrying the script. Paint him and you need his consent; paint hands only and you lose half the warmth. I would paint him and get the consent. (3) IT ABANDONS THE PROGRAMME'S ONLY UNIQUE ASSET. Nothing on this tray admits anything. No ceiling, no tolerance, no confession. Beside the austere pieces it can be dismissed as generic taqueria illustration, and that is the price of this territory, paid on purpose. (4) It puts YOUR hands in the frame, which tells the viewer they are the one being served. Some people find that presumptuous. (5) ON F92, NAMED BECAUSE FINDING 6 SAYS IT MUST BE: this piece is the emotional bar — 'the emotional bar that sits ABOVE clinical accuracy' — but it is NOT the Playa hero. It is set at the counter, needs no terrace, no palms, no `playa_env.py` and no second delivery frame. F92 says deprioritised, not cancelled, and do not act on it. This does not act on it.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Aperture 515.5 × 403.0 mm, r 55, at measured centres x +672 / +47 / −598 mm; sill 1372, head 1775 un-dropped (1307 / 1710 above ground). Counter top 1254 mm, bottom 1147 mm — a 107.0 mm slab, 321.0 mm deep (y 845 → 1166). Brass nosing 18.6 % of the slab's outer edge = 19.9 mm of gold in elevation, MEASURED over 113 columns at 4.52 ± 0.23 px, corroborated 0.191 on a second frame. Bobble fringe r 9 mm at 26 mm pitch, 13 mm inset. Galley strip-light emission 1.000 / 0.918 / 0.790; the backdrop's measured material seam at x −614 mm, white aft, warm cream forward. Brass linear (0.6600, 0.4750, 0.1750). UNMEASURED AND DRAWN ANYWAY, stated plainly: the hands, the plate, the food, and the galley's DEPTH — Y is chosen everywhere in this model and never measured, so any view into a bay is a construction.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Proof: dye-sublimation on 1 mm aluminium with a rolled polymer edge, one piece, 3–5 days, ~US$35 — indistinguishable from tin at arm's length. Real tin litho needs about 500 minimum on six plates: roughly US$2,800 setup and US$2.20 a tray at 1,000, US$1.60 at 5,000. The charolita shares the plates. The painting itself is 2–3 days of one illustrator working over a model-derived perspective grid. No new pipeline, no line pass, no render queue, nothing this project has to build first.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — One A4 painting of the hand-off, printed flat on matte stock, handed to him with nothing written on it and no explanation. If he smiles, it is a tray. If he corrects the food, it is a better tray. Either answer is worth more than another measurement.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:beautiful-2`

### DIEZ FLORES Y MEDIA  ·  screen 8.2/10

**REGISTER** polychrome — decorative, un-lettered, no line-work at all (territory: beautiful-not-rigorous)  
**ARTEFACT** A tablecloth for the restaurant's own tables. 1400 × 1400 mm, PVC-laminated cotton — the standard Mexican fonda hule, wipe-clean — printed in SEVEN inks on a near-black ground, plus a bleached-cotton napkin, 420 × 420 mm, from the same file. Half-drop repeat, no selvedge lettering, no logo, no dimension anywhere on it.

> The roof of the bus, un-tilted and laid flat, printed as an oilcloth for the tables: ten flower heads and a half, seven measured colours, and not one number.

A cloth for the tables, printed with the roof of the bus.

The mural board on the raised lid is the most beautiful object on this vehicle and the programme has not touched it once — eleven concepts of line and two inks, while the thing itself carries gold, orange and red on a ground that is almost black. This is that board, un-tilted, laid flat, and turned into a half-drop repeat: ten flower heads at their measured positions, eight petals each, the scalloped rings, the calligraphic C-scrolls and paisley hooks, the leaves. And the eleventh head — the half one, sliced by the right-hand menu strip — kept as a half, so that one flower on every cloth is cut off by nothing at all.

Through the flowers, the five little food vignettes lifted from the strips: two bolillos with green sprigs, a torta with pink meat and garnish, a tall pale glass beside an orange, a tostada, a single pink prawn.

No lettering. No bus. Not a figure anywhere.

Seven inks on a ground of 55, 26, 19 — the mottle at 74/38/26, then 159/45/22, 163/66/21, 172/119/24, 184/150/76, 206/118/124, 96/128/54. Those are the board's own colours, read off it at 8–22×. You put a plate down on it and eat, and nobody ever knows why one flower is a half.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The mural exists in ONE photograph, seen at LID_OPEN_DEG 76° and foreshortened. Copy it by eye and you copy a trapezoid. The model is the only thing that un-tilts it: the board's width is typed nowhere — it is derived from the opening angle and a scale-free measured aspect of 1.713, and I watched it print at LID_W = 1.2237 m — so the true 2034 × 1224 mm rectangle comes out of the geometry or it does not come out at all. The model also holds the ten head centroids in board-normalised (u,v), which reproduce to better than 0.012 in u and v across two independent passes, so the ARRANGEMENT on the cloth is the painter's and not a designer's memory of it. Nothing else in the artefact needs the model, and none of it shows.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — FOUR, none of them sanded. (1) SPEC §10.10 — 'ABSOLUTE REPLICATION OF ALL ARTWORK', his words, called a hard bar. This cloth deliberately breaks the mural's composition into a repeat. The rule-34 defence is that the bar's object is THE MODEL and a printed cloth is a different object — but that defence must be MADE to him, out loud, and he is entitled to say no. Finding 7 exists because three concepts did this silently. (2) AUTHORSHIP. The mural was painted by someone in Playa del Carmen whose name is nowhere in this repository. Putting their flowers into repeat and selling it is a real appropriation question, and the honest fix is not a disclaimer — it is finding the painter and crediting them, or commissioning a taller to redraw it, BEFORE volume. (3) The board's aspect has an honest bar of ±0.09, so any 'true size' claim would be a lie; the repeat is therefore set at a chosen pitch and no dimension goes on the face. (4) AESTHETIC. A floral oilcloth is the most ordinary object in every fonda in Mexico. If it is merely competent it vanishes. The entire bet is that these specific ten-and-a-half heads in these seven measured colours are not ordinary — and that bet can lose.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Lid opening 2034 mm (LID_X0 0.9640 → LID_X1 −1.0700); LID_W 1.2237 m DERIVED, watched print. Ten head centroids from lid_gen.HEADS_UV, plus HEADS_PARTIAL_UV (1.0050, 0.8460). 8 petals per rosette (k=8 power 42.6 against k=12 at 6.3; 6 of 7 heads give k=8 individually). Colours GROUND (55,26,19), GROUND_HI (74,38,26), RED (159,45,22), ORANGE (163,66,21), GOLD (172,119,24), PALE (184,150,76), PINK (206,118,124), GREEN (96,128,54) — all MEASURED in-situ over 2,569,748 px. Strip fractions STRIP_L_F 0.0835 / STRIP_R_F 0.0794 place the cut on the half head. DELIBERATELY NOT USED: BOARD_ASPECT 1.654 as a true-size claim (honest bar ±0.09); INK (72,46,6) and PEACE (198,172,122), both CONSTRUCTED, not measured; the retired bay taper.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Digital textile proof on PVC-coated cotton, 1 m², 48–72 h, ~US$40–80. A short run of 20 cloths digitally printed, ~US$25 each, no setup. Screen-printed hule at 500 m: seven screens, ~US$1,200 setup, then ~US$4/m — the standard Mexican hule trade does exactly this and nothing about it is exotic. Napkins from the same file on a cotton sateen, ~US$6 each at 100. The artwork is two days: `lid_gen.py` already draws every element procedurally in board-normalised coordinates, so the repeat is a re-tiling of code that exists, not a redraw.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — One 600 × 600 mm swatch of the repeat, digitally printed on oilcloth, in his hands this week. Lay it on a table, put a plate of food on it, photograph it from standing height. That is the entire test and it needs no explanation: does he want to eat off it.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:beautiful-1`

### GUIRNALDA  ·  screen 8.03/10

**REGISTER** three-dimensional / environmental — an object that makes warm light, no line-work, no text (territory: beautiful-not-rigorous)  
**ARTEFACT** A pendant lampshade for the restaurant's own tables. Drum, Ø199 mm × 340 mm, translucent styrene-backed parchment, two opaque screen inks (one warm red, one cream), with 118 laser perforations along the top edge. LED only. Sixty of them, one over each table.

> A pendant shade the flank wraps exactly once at 1:6.5; switch it on and three serving hatches and 118 festoon lamps come alight over every table.

Sixty little buses, lit, one over every table.

A drum shade, 199 mm across and 340 mm tall. The flank of the vehicle wraps it exactly once at 1:6.5 — 4065 mm of body becomes 625 mm of circumference — so the nose meets the tail at the seam and the bus closes into a ring. Two opaque inks, one warm red and one cream, screen-printed on translucent parchment laminate. Everything that is a hole or a pane is simply left unprinted. Round the top edge, 118 perforations at 4.39 mm pitch: one per lamp, the drip-rail festoon at its own realised pitch reduced six and a half times.

Off, it is a plain red drum with a cream band and a fine line of pinholes. A decent object, nothing more.

On, three warm rectangles open in the side, the windscreen and the door glass come up, and 118 points of light run right round the top. It is the bus at dusk with its hatches propped and its string lit — the state that is unmistakable in two of the photographs, that the render cannot reproduce because the view transform takes the warmth out of a lamp at that brightness, and that this project has therefore never once shown anybody.

Because the flank wraps exactly once there is no second side to draw. That is convenient, because nobody has ever photographed the other side of this vehicle.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — This is the one of the three that cannot be faked. It needs a true orthographic side elevation at an exact reduction — and the side ortho camera is the one that already FITS today (5.90 → 4.0563 m against a 4.065 m body), while front and rear clip. It needs the three aperture stations, the arch lines, the belt at 1207 mm, the 17.75 mm/m rake and the 5.5 mm shut lines all correct at 1:6.5, where a caricature reads as a caricature instantly and from across a room. And it needs the 118 lamp positions, which are a build-time derivation from BULB_X0 −1.6368, BULB_X1 +1.7000 and a 28.6 mm measured pitch — not a countable feature of any photograph, where only about six bulbs resolve. Every die-cast, every LEGO set, every T-shirt silhouette of this vehicle has the nose and the roof wrong. This would be the first one that does not, and the mesh is the only reason it can be.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — (1) HEAT AND FIRE, AND IT IS FIRST FOR A REASON. A perforated paper drum over a lamp in a working restaurant is a genuine hazard. LED only, ≤ 6 W, ≥ 60 mm clearance, and the shade must be built by a lighting manufacturer to CE/UL with a report, not run off by a print shop. If we cannot get that certificate it does not go in a room, full stop, and I would rather kill it than fudge it. (2) TRADE DRESS. A recognisable T1 silhouette on a manufactured lamp is precisely what Volkswagen's licensing people look for, and composing the roundel out does not help — THE SHAPE IS THE MARK. Made for Tacombi's own rooms it is decor and defensible; sold to the public it needs a licence or a lawyer, and that has to be settled before tooling, not after. (3) IT IS ONE STEP FROM THE BUS-SHAPED COOKIE JAR. The only thing separating this from a gift-shop novelty is that the elevation is honest and the reds are the measured ones. Let the printer drift the red toward tomato and it becomes a novelty overnight; there is no defence but proofing. (4) 340 mm of drop over a table is a real spatial decision in rooms nobody on this project has stood in, and the ceiling height in either site is UNMEASURED. (5) ON FINDING 8, DECLARED RATHER THAN ROUTED AROUND: the VW roundel never appears on this object, because the nose lands edge-on in the seam. That is a choice made knowing the emblem has been rejected nine times and F191 still stands. I am declaring it, not hoping nobody notices.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Body length 4065 mm; roof at the rear axle 1983.5 mm (n = 230 mesh samples). Three apertures 515.5 × 403.0, r 55, at measured centres x +672 / +47 / −598 mm. Belt line 1207 mm above ground; window band 1307–1710; rake 17.75 mm/m; shut lines 5.5 mm; wheelbase 2400; track 1369 / 1359; tyre ⌀664.9; hubcap ⌀274.0. Festoon run 3336.8 mm carrying 118 lamps at a realised 28.52 mm pitch — watched print: 117 segments over BULB_X0 −1.6368 to BULB_X1 +1.7000 at BULB_PITCH 0.0286. AT 1:6.5 those become 625.4 mm circumference, 305.2 mm of roof height, 79.3 × 62.0 mm hatches at r 8.5, and 118 holes at 4.39 mm over 513.4 mm. NOTE AND CORRECTION: the circulating figure of '115 festoon lamps' is a photograph-side INFERENCE about a 3.50 m rail, not this model — the build makes 118. UNMEASURED: the off flank, grade E, never photographed — avoided by construction here, not solved. UNMEASURED: the ceiling height in either room.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — One hand-made prototype — high-resolution print or screen on 0.25 mm translucent styrene laminate, laser-perforated, rolled and seamed onto Ø199 mm top and bottom rings — about US$120–180 and 7–10 days from a small shade maker. Production for 60 units: roughly US$3,000 tooling including a lighting-safety report, then about US$28 a unit. The artwork file is one afternoon once a side orthographic render exists, and the side ortho camera works today. It needs no line pass, no Grease Pencil route, no new export path.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Skip the shade entirely. Print the flank artwork at 1:6.5 on a sheet of tracing paper, roll it into a Ø199 mm tube with tape, drop it over a battery LED candle, turn the lights off and photograph it. Ninety minutes and three dollars, and it answers the only question that matters: does it glow the way you hope.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — False  ·  **id** `dream:beautiful-3`

## THE CHILD'S EYE

### ERA VERDE — la combi que era verde  ·  screen 7.33/10

**REGISTER** child · polychrome · food-and-hand-off (CHILDREN'S LINE, F331)  
**ARTEFACT** A3 placemat, 420 × 297 mm, uncoated 120 gsm, printed both sides. FOUR spot inks — #608036 (declared as the body green; it is measured as the menu vignettes' garnish green), #371A13 (mural ground, line and type), #CE767C (vignette pink), #AC9011 (menu-strip yellow). The FIFTH ink is a red wax crayon and the restaurant already owns it.

> The bus is printed green with three empty holes; the child paints it red and draws the food and the people the programme could not.

FRONT. The bus at EXACTLY 1:10 from the true orthographic side — 406.5 mm long, 198.3 mm to the roof over the rear axle, which fits A3 with 6.75 mm each end. Printed flat green with the line pass over it. The three serving holes are bare paper at 51.55 × 40.3 mm, r 5.5, genuinely equal because the mesh says so. Two instructions, big: PÍNTALA ROJA / PAINT IT RED, and DIBUJA LO QUE VA EN LOS TRES HUECOS. Under it, four short facts: it was green, it cost $3,000, three of its windows were already missing, it opened on 6 February 2006. Then the honest one, in a child's words: nobody wrote down why there are three holes; we think it is because three windows were already gone; we are not sure, and that is a real answer.

BACK — CUÉNTALOS. Six counting boxes with our number, our doubt, and a blank: we drew 10 louvres and can only see 7 in the photograph — how many can you see? 118 lamps on the roof edge at 28.52 mm. 26 bulbs on the tail board, of which 6 show. 8 petals a flower, 10 flowers and a half. 65 white bobbles round one hole — INFERRED; NOBODY HAS EVER COUNTED THEM. The five measured menu vignettes — bolillos, the pink-and-green torta, the tall glass and the orange, the tostada, the prawn — sit between the boxes at 8×, the first real food this programme has printed. Name and date at the foot. Hand it back at the counter.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The true orthographic side elevation at an exact 1:10 with no lens, which no photograph of this vehicle can give; the line pass (`line_pass.py`, built rev 77) for the drawing; three apertures drawn equal because the mesh is equal; and occlusion into the empty holes so the child's crayon lands inside a real box rather than on a flat silhouette. The green is a MATERIAL switch, not new geometry — we take the paint off today's bus. We hold no photograph of THIS bus green; that is stated on the sheet.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Three, none sanded off. (1) It sends children to look closely at a 1963 vehicle standing in a working dining room — Nolita's bus is parked among the tables — and a placemat that says COUNT THE BOBBLES will produce a child at the fringe with a finger out. That is a service and insurance question, not a design one, and it is the owner's to accept or refuse. (2) It accepts a seven-year-old's count as evidence. Either that is the best thing this project has done — three independent children agreeing beats a 4.5 px photograph under a JPEG block — or it is a farce, and I cannot tell you which until the sheets come back. Grade any count OBSERVATION, n = children, and never overwrite a mesh figure with one. (3) PAINT IT RED tells a child what colour to use. A designer will object that this is anti-creative. Kept deliberately: the green-to-red transformation is the founder's own act and the child performs it. On SPEC §10.10's artwork bar — the green state has no mural, no script, no sunburst to replicate, so the bar is honoured by chronology rather than waived. The nose roundel is composed out by the side projection, not by choice; F191 untouched.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Trivially feasible: `sheet.py` already writes mm-accurate SVG and a matching PNG proof, and the side line pass exists. Litho at 500+ is cents a sheet; the test run costs the restaurant's own printer and a ream of paper. UNQUOTED — get three quotes before printing 5,000. The only build work is turning the decal materials off and one flat-fill pass.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Print 20 on the restaurant's own A3 printer this week, put them on the tables at Nolita for one lunch service with the crayons already there, and keep every sheet a child drew on. He is handed twenty drawings, not a mock-up. If three children draw a taco in the middle hole, the concept is proven; if they draw a dinosaur, that is worth knowing too.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:child-1`

### MI COMBI — the die-cut sticker, finally built (F18)  ·  screen 7.27/10

**REGISTER** child · polychrome · packaging (CHILDREN'S LINE, F331)  
**ARTEFACT** Kiss-cut white matte vinyl sticker, 101.6 × 78.3 mm (the bus at 1:40, mural lid UP, in service), die line taken straight from the line pass's outer contour. Six colours: #C43124 red, #CED0C8 cream, #AC7718 gold, #A34215 orange, #371A13 ground, #AC9011 strip yellow. Kiss-cut on a 120 × 100 mm backing card that is itself a cut-paper sheet, perforated so it can be hung.

> The project's original deliverable, open since rev 44 and unblocked by him at rev 77 — the bus alone, cartoon with rendered depth, at 1:40.

He locked the style eleven years of revisions ago and it is a render recipe, not a mood: vector line and flat colour, shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset. Half of it now exists. `line_pass.py` gives the vector line and, in the same file, the closed cut path a vinyl cutter wants — a die-cut sticker and papel picado are the same operation. The missing half is one AO pass at the same camera, quantised to ONE shadow tint per flat colour. No gradients: flat colour is locked.

The wheels get his own instruction back. He said he liked how they were drawn in the earlier cartoon version, and the machine says that artefact is not here — 793 commits, eight hits, all markdown. UNRECOVERED. So the wheel is drawn as MEASURED circles by `la_rueda.py`'s route, which cross-checks four independent quantities: tyre 664.9, rim flange 439.6, hubcap 274.0, badge 86.9, agreeing to −0.8 / +0.6 / −0.2 / +0.5 mm. A cartoon wheel that is the most-measured object in the programme.

His scene lock names three things: the bus, the sun, the papel picado. The record measures the last two as absent from the reference. He deferred that — 'I'll decide when the sticker is actually being built.' It is now being built, so the deferral's own condition is met and the answer is not a question but two printed stickers: A puts the sun and the cut paper on the CARRIER, B puts them inside the die line.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Total dependency, and it is the only artefact in the programme whose STYLE the owner himself specified as a render recipe. The line pass IS the drawing and IS the die line — one file feeds the printer and the cutter. The AO pass is the 'rendered depth' clause and has to come off the asset; nothing else in this project can produce it. The hubcap's rings fragment in Line Art (F333) and are recovered as true circles from a radius histogram, so the wheel is traced where tracing works and measured where it does not, and the key says which is which.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — I am shipping a variant that DOES NOT MATCH HIS LOCKED SCENE, and that is stated on the face of the proof rather than smuggled. Variant A drops two of the three elements he named out of the artwork; if he meant them in the artwork, variant B is already in the same envelope and he only has to point. Second risk: the second half of his own sentence does not exist yet — there is no AO or normal pass in 77 revisions — so a sticker shipped before it is built is vector line and flat colour with the depth missing, which is exactly the half-built thing he would spot. Do not ship the flat version and call it done. Third: this object contains no food and no people, by his lock, in a programme a critic has just faulted for exactly that. That is a declaration, not an oversight. Fourth: at 1:40 the nose roundel is 7 mm and the hubcap badge 2.2 mm — below legibility, so the mark he has rejected nine times is present and unresolvable. F333 observed that the badge traces CLEANLY in line, which is interesting and is not a claim the emblem is fixed. F191 and F234 stand.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — The cheapest artefact in the programme. Kiss-cut short-run vinyl is tens of dollars for 250 — UNQUOTED, three quotes. One day of build work for the AO pass and the flat-fill separation; `sheet.py` already writes the SVG master and the cutter takes SVG directly. No new geometry.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Cut ONE on a desktop vinyl cutter this week and hand him three things in an envelope: variant A (bus alone, sun and cut paper on the card), variant B (his locked scene entire), and a strip of three wheel treatments. One sentence with it: 'You said you liked how the wheels were drawn before. We cannot find that drawing. Point at one.'

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — False  ·  **id** `dream:child-2`

### ¿YA ALCANZAS? — the bus as a height chart  ·  screen 6.13/10

**REGISTER** child · environmental · people · food-and-hand-off (CHILDREN'S LINE, F331)  
**ARTEFACT** TWO objects. (1) A 1:1 vinyl strip, 2100 × 220 mm, fixed to the wall beside the parked bus. Three inks: cream #CED0C8 ground, red #C43124 rules, #371A13 type. (2) A 90 × 55 mm card, two inks, uncoated, with blanks for HEIGHT (mm), DATE and NAME, and the bus's own heights printed down one side.

> Every line on the chart is a real part of the bus, and the line that matters is 1312 mm — the height at which the food is handed to you.

A child at a taqueria spends the wait looking up. The counter is above their eye and the hubcap is below it. So make the bus the ruler.

The marks, all above ground, all watched print this session: 332.5 mm the middle of the hubcap, which is the tyre's own radius. 665 the top of the tyre. THE COUNTER 1190 at the front and 1245 at the tail — it is not level, because the bus stands nose-down at 17.75 mm per metre, so the counter climbs 55 mm as it runs back. THE THREE HOLES, sills at 1312, 1323, 1335, front to back, for the same reason. Their heads at 1715, 1726, 1738. And at the top, in 40% tint with an open bracket: 1983 mm, THE ROOF IN THE MODEL. THE REAL ROOF HAS NEVER BEEN MEASURED. BRING A TAPE.

1312 is the one with a sentence under it: WHEN YOU REACH THIS LINE YOU CAN SEE INTO THE KITCHEN. It is where food crosses.

The card is filled in by the cook and handed out through the hole — the transaction the whole brand is, performed on the child. It carries the measurement in the house register: MEASURED BY ____ · CEILING ± 5 mm, one tape, one child, standing straight. Families bring it back. That is his own bar: something families keep.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Partial, and I will not fake it. A tape measure and STATE.md would give most of these numbers. What the model contributes that nothing else can: the frame discipline that makes them honest — the counter reads 1190 at the front and 1245 at the tail ONLY because build.py subtracts the ride drop per-vertex by station, and no tape held against a parked bus would tell you the 55 mm is rake rather than error; and the 1:1 silhouette edge for the strip. Its best contribution is not a number but a refusal: it is the only thing in the room that knows 1983 is the MODEL's roof and that the real one is open.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — It quantifies a child, on a wall, in public. Some children are anxious about being small and some parents will find a number cold. Mitigation is real but partial: every line is a bus part, there is no percentile and no age, and the chart tops out at a hole rather than at 'tall'. Second: it assumes a physical bus is present and reachable — grounded for Nolita, where the bus stands in the room among the tables; UNKNOWN everywhere else, and that assumption was one the last round made silently. Third: a permanent-ish vinyl on a wall needs the venue's permission and is not a designer's call. Fourth, and this one cuts toward us: the chart invites somebody to put a tape on the real roof, and if it reads 1930 or 2050 the model moves and 77 revisions acquire a correction they did not ask for. That is a gift and it is a risk, and hiding it would be the one unacceptable move.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — A 2.1 m printed vinyl panel is a sign-shop job — order of a hundred dollars, UNQUOTED. Cards are pennies. No new build work: every figure exists, and `sheet.py` writes the strip as an SVG a sign shop can print directly. The only genuine dependency is a wall next to the bus.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — This week: 2 m of plotter paper taped to the wall beside the bus with the eight lines drawn on it, and ten cards off the office printer. Run it for one service. Photograph one child standing against 1312 with the card in their hand. That single photograph is the whole argument, and it costs nothing.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:child-3`

## FREE DIRECTOR 1 — no territory

### EL FIERRO  ·  screen 8.5/10

**REGISTER** three-dimensional / working tool / food and packaging — an identity with ZERO ink  
**ARTEFACT** A branding iron. Face CNC-cut from 8 mm brass at 90 × 44 mm, silver-brazed to a 6 mm steel shank and an ash handle; a second face at 120 × 59 mm for paper. Heated on the plancha the kitchen already owns. Four substrates: the deli paper the food is handed over on, the wooden serving board, a leather apron patch, and — the risky one — the tortilla itself. Ink count: zero. Print run: none. There is no file to send.

> The identity is not a file. It is a hot piece of brass, and the last thing that happens to your food before it crosses the counter.

Every mark this brand could make already exists as vinyl. A fierro is the opposite: a registered ranch mark, one closed line, burned. It cannot be resized in a browser, it cannot be emailed, and no two impressions are the same.

The face is the vehicle's true side elevation reduced until it survives a burn: outline, the arch cuts, and the only interior counters that earn their place — the three serving apertures, 515.5 × 403.0 mm, r 55, EQUAL, dropped straight off the mesh. At 90 mm long those become 11.4 × 8.9 mm holes with r 1.2 corners, which is at the edge of what char will hold. The model is how you find that out before you cut brass.

It is used at the pass. The cook lays the paper down, presses, the paper smokes for half a second in front of the customer, the food goes on it and crosses the counter. The mark is made in front of the person it is made for, by the hand that cooked, at the moment of the hand-off. That is the brand's actual emotional transaction and no poster contains it.

Engraved small on the shank: 4065 × 1750 · ROOF UNMEASURED. The model reads 1983.3 mm at the rear axle; the real vehicle's absolute roof height is open (H_ROOF retired, rev 22). The tool carries its own ceiling.

Composes the VW roundel out entirely — F191 is untouched, and the roundel is VW's trademark anyway. Replicates no artwork: a burn has no colour. SPEC 10.10 governs the model; this is a different object, and the omission is declared, not routed around.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Three things only the mesh gives. (1) A true orthographic side silhouette — the side ortho camera is built and fits (ortho scale 5.90, read in studio.py this session; front and rear sit at 3.55 and clip). Every VW bus silhouette in the world is a caricature with a round nose and a tall roof; this is the chance to burn the first accurate one, and at 90 mm the difference is the whole concept. (2) The aperture rectangles at their true positions and the arch radii — the mark's only interior counters, so their geometry IS the design. (3) Legibility testing at burn scale: render the reduction at 90, 55 and 25 mm and watch the counters close before committing brass. A photograph supplies none of this; the record grades the off side E, never photographed, and the on side has a man leaning across the front arch in the primary frame.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Four, none removable. (a) A hot iron in a working kitchen with children in the room; one photograph of a kid near it is a problem, and a health inspector will ask about scorched paper. (b) The livestock connotation is real — a fierro marks ownership on an animal, and applying it to food will read badly to some people, particularly in a Mexican-American context where the reference is not decorative. (c) Char is uncontrollable: masa moisture, iron temperature, dwell time. A fraction of every batch will be blobs and there is no QC. The inconsistency is the charm and it is also the defect, and he may hate it. (d) Prior art exists — steakhouses brand buns. If the reduction is weak it reads as a generic hippie van at 90 mm, and this project's entire accuracy claim dies on the object that carries it most publicly.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Body 4065 mm long, 1750 wide (build.py `dims L=4.065 W=1.750`). Serving apertures 515.5 × 403.0 mm, r 55, THREE, EQUAL — read out of t1_shell.py this session: `BAY_W = 0.5155  # equal, measured`, `Z_SILL, Z_HEAD, BAY_R = 1.3720, 1.7750, 0.0550`. Tyre outside diameter 664.9 mm (`TIRE_R = 0.3325`, rev-6 measured, NOT a 6.40-15). Rear arch radius 373.5 mm. Rocker to ground 317.7 mm. Rake 17.75 mm/m (`RAKE_DZDX = 0.017750`). Roof at rear axle 1983.3 mm, n = 230 mesh samples — engraved beside the word UNMEASURED, because the real vehicle's roof height is open. The 90 / 55 / 25 mm face sizes are CHOICES, not measurements, and are labelled as such.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Test face laser-cut in 3 mm mild steel: $40–80, next day at any laser shop. Production face CNC'd in 8 mm brass with brazed shank and turned handle: $180–350 from a trophy or branding-iron maker, two weeks. Bench-top electric branding press if it goes to volume: $150–600. Substrates are already in the building; consumables are zero. The only real cost is the reduction itself — a designer's week and a set of test burns.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Laser-cut the 90 mm reduction in 3 mm mild steel this week, about $50. Heat it on his own plancha and burn ten sheets of the deli paper he already buys, one wooden board, and three tortillas. Lay the ten sheets side by side and photograph them. That is the entire decision — he either loves that no two are the same or he does not — and it costs under $100 to find out.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-1-1`

### DALE, DALE, DALE  ·  screen 7.83/10

**REGISTER** three-dimensional / saturated polychrome / children / commissioned craft — and it is destroyed  
**ARTEFACT** A piñata of this vehicle at 1:5 — 813.0 mm long, 350.0 wide, 396.7 to the roof — commissioned from a named piñatería, built over a cardboard armature and surfaced in cut papel de china fringe in the measured mural palette. The three serving apertures are left open as three real holes, 103.1 × 80.6 mm, r 11. That is where the contents come out. Payload: 200 die-cut vinyl stickers of the bus, 75 mm across, cut tight to the silhouette. F18, the oldest live row in the register, open since rev 44, finally made.

> The bus at one-fifth in cut tissue, filled with the sticker we have owed him for thirty-two revisions — and the children break it open and the food comes out of the serving hatches.

Seventy-six revisions have gone into never getting this object wrong. This is the first one built to be destroyed, and that is not a joke at the project's expense — it is the correct register for a taqueria and it is his own bar, in his own words: spark joy, something families keep.

Two things stop it being fiesta tat. First, it is accurate. Every piñata, die-cast and T-shirt silhouette of a Type 2 ever made is a caricature; this one has the cut-open roof, the nose-down 1.02° stance and three EQUAL holes where the food comes out. Second, it is commissioned, not imitated — a piñatería builds it and is credited on the swing tag, which is the only honest answer to the appropriation question.

What comes out is the point. Not sweets: 200 stickers, die-cut tight, drawn in the style he locked himself — vector line and flat colour, shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset. The papel picado he ruled open stays open: it ships as a separate switchable layer for the day he decides, exactly as he asked. I am not re-putting that question.

It does not replicate the artwork. Tissue fringe cannot hold the mural, the script or the Calidad sunburst; the mural becomes a colour field in its own measured hexes. SPEC 10.10's hard bar governs the model, not a derivative object — declared. No roundel anywhere on it.

And it asks the one question the list missed: the sticker's own trigger reads build it after the model is done, and the model is not done. Does rev 76's redirect override his own deferral? Two options, one sentence, his call.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Less than it looks, and I would rather say so. A good piñatero could build a beautiful one from a photograph and would not thank me for CAD nets. What the model buys is accuracy the craft cannot reach alone: the true silhouette, the raked stance at 17.75 mm/m, the roof lids at 76.0° open, and the three aperture positions so the holes are where the holes are. The nets are offered, not imposed. Where the model is genuinely load-bearing is the sticker inside it — 'shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset' is his own locked style, and it requires the Grease Pencil Line Art and AO passes that do not exist in this tree yet. Building them is the keystone item, and this concept is what pays for it and what ships through it.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — (a) You hit it with a stick until it breaks. He may find a precisely measured recreation of his bus being smashed by children upsetting rather than delightful, and there is no way to learn that except by showing him. I am not softening this into a display piece — a piñata that is not broken is a decoration, and then the concept is dead. (b) Piñata is the third rail after the sugar skull and the lucha mask; it can read as bulk Mexicana, and the only defence is a named taller, credited. (c) It is the one concept here that deletes his artwork, and pretending tissue could carry the mural would be worse. (d) A child swinging a stick in a dining room full of plates. (e) The sticker's deferral trigger is unmet and I am proposing to override it — that is his ruling to make, and if he says wait, the piñata ships with sweets and the sticker waits.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Everything scaled 1:5 off measured figures: 813.0 mm long (4065/5), 350.0 wide (1750/5), 396.7 to the roof at the rear axle (1983.3/5 — and the swing tag says the real roof height is UNMEASURED). Apertures 103.1 × 80.6 mm, r 11, three, EQUAL (515.5 × 403.0, r 55). Tyre 133.0 mm (664.9/5). Rake 17.75 mm/m, so it stands nose-down 1.02° and the piñata should too. Roof lid open 76.0° — DECLARED, inside a 68.9–78° bracket, not measured; the tag says so. Mural palette measured in situ off the rectified board and read out of lid_gen.py this session: ground #371A13, ground-highlight #4A261A, red #9F2D16, orange #A34215, gold #AC7718, pale #B8964C; body red #C43124, cream #CED0C8. Ten full flower heads plus one half head cut by the right strip; eight petals, not twelve (k=8 power 42.6 against k=12 at 6.3).

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — A commissioned shaped piñata from a taller: $80–250 each, two to four weeks, less per unit in a run of 25. Sticker die plus 500 kiss-cut vinyl stickers: $150–300, about ten days. The 1:20 paper maquette: one sheet of A3 and an evening. A shippable season is under $2,000. The long pole is not money — it is the sticker artwork, which needs the line and AO passes built first: three to five days on the model that the whole programme needs anyway and that no other concept has yet paid for.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Two objects in his hands this week. (1) A 1:20 paper maquette, 203 mm long, printed on one A3 sheet as a cut-and-fold net and assembled in an evening, so he can hold the silhouette and see whether it reads as his bus. (2) Twenty stickers, printed and kiss-cut same-day locally from a first pass of the line drawing, before the AO pass exists. Put both on the counter in Nolita and watch what a child does with them. That is the test, and it is a Tuesday.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-1-2`

### ¿CUÁNTO MIDES?  ·  screen 5.33/10

**REGISTER** environmental / people / ritual — a measuring instrument in the dining room, and a card families keep  
**ARTEFACT** A station rail: 2000 × 40 mm, 3 mm anodised aluminium, engraved to one depth, fixed vertically to the wall beside the bus — or freestanding on a folded-steel foot where there is no bus. It carries the vehicle's measured above-ground stations as engraved lines, each with its figure in millimetres and the letters AG, and above the top line an open bracket. Beside it, a card: 105 × 148 mm, one ink on warm uncoated stock, carrying a date, a name, a height in millimetres, and which part of the bus the child reached.

> The bus is the ruler. A child stands against it and is recorded in millimetres against a vehicle whose own roof height this project refuses to publish.

REF_MEASUREMENTS.md, cross-checking the scale of ref_side.jpg, line 78: "the leaning man measures 375 px = 1.78 m at 211 px/m (he is slightly nearer the camera, so his true height is a little under that — right for an adult male)." A human being was measured, correctly, with a stated ceiling — and then used as a ruler check and thrown away. Elsewhere a probe discarded three points because they landed on a child's hair. Forty revisions have treated the people at this bus as contamination.

The rail turns the instrument around. Its lines are stations the machine will stand behind, above ground: rocker 317.7, top of tyre 664.9, belt line 1207.0, window sill 1307.0, window head 1710.0, roof at the rear axle 1983.3 — and then nothing, because H_ROOF was retired at rev 22 and the real roof height is open. The top of a children's height chart is an admission of ignorance.

And it carries the fact that undoes every level line in the room: this bus is raked 17.75 mm per metre. The belt line is 1207.0 mm above ground at x = +962 and 1224.1 mm at mid-wheelbase. There is no single answer to how high the window sill is. The rail states where its figures are true and tells you they rise 17.75 mm for every metre you walk toward the tail. The counter you are served across is not level either — it falls 55.1 mm end to end.

This does not act on F92; the Playa hero stays deprioritised. But it is aimed at his sentence: the emotional bar that sits above clinical accuracy.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The model is the only source of a self-consistent above-ground station set, and the trap it resolves is a 65 mm one: t1_core, t1_shell and t1_detail constants are authored UN-DROPPED and build.py subtracts the ride drop last, while the shader constants are already above ground. Engrave the counter's authored 1254 as a height and you are 28–71 mm wrong depending where along the bus you stand. The mesh also supplies the rake, which is what makes every station a function of x — the machine's own z_belt(x) = Z_BELT0 − 0.017750·x, which I ran this session. You could and should also put a tape on the real Nolita bus; the model tells you in advance which figures exist, which move with x, and which the record refuses to publish. The human ghost ticks need no model at all — they need photogrammetry on frames the model calibrates.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — The ghost ticks are the danger and I am not removing them. Engraving 'a child, magenta puffer coat' at her measured height on a public wall means publishing data derived from a real, identifiable child's likeness with no release, from a photograph whose provenance I have not established. The leaning man and the woman with her chin on her hand are strangers too. Tacombi may own the frames; that is not consent. Strip the ghosts and it becomes a nursery height chart — the exact cliché this must avoid — so the risk and the idea are the same object and cannot be separated. Second: it is a permanent 2 m graphic in his dining room, the only concept here that touches the fabric of the restaurant. Third: measuring children in public, and the temptation to collect the data. Fourth: every human height except the leaning man's is UNMEASURED today, and his carries the record's own 'a little under that'.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — All above ground, all from the machine this session. Rocker to ground 317.7. Top of tyre 664.9 (TYRE_D 0.6649). Belt line 1207.0 at x = +962 and 1224.1 at x = 0 — computed live from t1_mats' own `Z_BELT_AUTH 1.2720 − RAKE_Z0 0.047925` and `z_belt(x) = Z_BELT0 − RAKE_DZDX·x`. Window sill 1307.0, head 1710.0 (Z_SILL 1.3720 / Z_HEAD 1.7750 un-dropped, converted in t1_shell's own comment). Roof at rear axle 1983.3, n = 230. Rake 17.75 mm/m; ride drop 47.9 at x = 0, 71.0 / 28.4 at front / rear axle. The leaning man 1.78 m, with the record's own ceiling attached. DERIVED, NOT MACHINE-PRINTED, and marked so on the rail: the counter falls 55.1 mm across its own 3.106 m — I computed it this session from CNT_X0 0.9180, CNT_X1 −2.188 and RAKE_DZDX 0.017750; nothing in the tree prints it. UNMEASURED: the real vehicle's roof height, and every human height but one.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Engraved anodised rail, 2000 × 40 mm: $120–250 each from any trophy or signage engraver, about five days; ten sites for under $1,500. Cards: 500 at A6, one colour — $60 digital, $250–400 letterpress. Folded-steel foot for the freestanding version: $80. The photogrammetry that turns the ghost ticks from brackets into figures is one session's work on frames already on the repo, using the established scale of 211.5 ± 1.3 px/m for ref_side.jpg. If it will not resolve better than ±40 mm, the ticks stay brackets and the rail says so on its face.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Plot the rail at 1:1 on a 2 m paper strip, about $25, same day. Tape it to a wall in Nolita beside the actual bus, measure three children with a tape, write their cards by hand. Photograph one child standing at the engraved line that reads 1207.0 mm BELT LINE. That single photograph is the whole pitch and it costs twenty-five dollars.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-1-3`
