## FREE DIRECTOR 2 — look away from the bus

### DIRECTO  ·  screen 8.17/10

**REGISTER** environmental / hand-painted object — signage made by a named human, no ink count, no vector, no print  
**ARTEFACT** Hand-lettered automotive enamel on 1.2 mm sheet steel. TWO objects: (a) the windscreen board, 585.0 × 377.0 mm with a 50 mm corner, sized to sit inside one pane of the split screen of the actual bus parked in the Nolita room; (b) the wall board, 900 × 300 mm, one per site, hung by the pass. Four to six colours, whatever the painter mixes. Signed and dated on the reverse by the rotulista.

> In Mexico a combi is not a hippie van, it is a bus that works for a living and carries a hand-painted route board in its windscreen — so give this one its board, and let it read the truth.

A pesero — combi, micro — is a privately run minibus. The name IS the fare: one peso. From the 1980s the operators ran VW Microbuses for their twelve seats, and every one carries a hand-painted destination placard propped in the windscreen. That is the word Dario Wolos took for the brand. The English-speaking VW world has flower power and surfboards; the Mexican referent is a vehicle that works a shift, and this bus is literally that vehicle put back to work.

It has never had its board. It sits in a room on Elizabeth Street with an empty windscreen.

The board reads the actual itinerary, stacked in the pesero idiom, the important stop biggest:

CIUDAD DE MÉXICO · YUCATÁN (MUY DESPACIO) · PLAYA DEL CARMEN · [POR BARCO] · NUEVA YORK

Along the foot, small: TACOS · TORTAS · JUGOS, with one painted food vignette copied from the bus's own menu strips. Top left, where a pesero puts it: DIRECTO. It was not direct. It took a peninsula very slowly and then a boat, and the joke is the founder's own word — "wheeled it slowly (very slowly) through the Yucatan peninsula."

Painted by a working rotulista, commissioned, credited and PAID. Rotulismo is called el otro muralismo and it is dying under printed vinyl. Buying four boards from four painters is the cheapest possible act of not being a company that Photoshops a Mexican-look font.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Almost nothing, and I would rather say so than fake a dependency. ONE dimension: the windscreen board must sit inside one pane, and only the mesh holds it. `t1_shell.py` carries `WS_PANE_W, WS_PANE_H, WS_DIV, WS_R = 0.5850, 0.3770, 0.0260, 0.050` — read live off source this session, with NO provenance comment beside it, so it is NOT GRADED MEASURED. Verify it against the actual bus before anyone cuts steel. Across both panes plus the divider derives to 1196.0 mm. Everything else about this object is a painter, a brush and a can of enamel. Seventy-six revisions of geometry contribute one number, and that fact belongs in front of the owner rather than hidden.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — ONE: IT IS COSTUME. This bus was never a pesero and never ran a route. A destination board on a vehicle that did not do public transport can read as a hospitality brand borrowing working-class iconography it did not earn. The board's own text is the only defence — it confesses the boat and it confesses MUY DESPACIO — and if anyone ever "cleans that up" into a straight three-city route the object becomes a lie and should be scrapped, not shipped. TWO: APPROPRIATION. Rotulismo, papel picado, Talavera and lotería are living practices with living practitioners and, in cases, legal protection. The only defensible relationship is commission–credit–pay. If the budget is cut to a printed "rotulista-style" vinyl board, KILL THIS CONCEPT rather than value-engineer it. THREE: THE ADDRESS. SPEC §7 states 251 Elizabeth Street; `IMG_3840.jpeg` read at 9× says 267. That contradiction is live and unresolved in this repository. DO NOT PAINT AN ADDRESS ON STEEL UNTIL SOMEONE READS THE DOOR. FOUR, aimed at us: this is the most vivid object I have and it needs one number out of seventy-six revisions of measurement. If the owner asks what the model was for, the honest answer for this artefact is "the width of a windscreen," and he is entitled to find that annoying. FIVE: no roundel appears — the board is not the bus — so F191 is untouched. Declared.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Windscreen pane 585.0 × 377.0 mm, divider 26.0, corner radius 50.0 — read live off `t1_shell.py` this session, PROVENANCE UNSTATED IN SOURCE, therefore not graded measured. Derived: the pair across both panes plus divider = 1196.0 mm. Lettering vocabulary read off the reference frames: the lid rail TACOMBI · NUEVA YORK · PLAYA DEL CARMEN, and TACOS · TORTAS · JUGOS from the menu strips; the painted food vignette is copied from the bus's own strip vignettes (PINK 206/118/124, GREEN 96/128/54, CREAMV 232/224/198). SECONDARY AND FLAGGED ON THE FACE OF THIS CONCEPT: the pesero one-peso etymology, the founder's $3,000 green bus with three windows missing, and the 6 February 2006 opening are web-search and dossier figures, not primary sources — re-check every one before it is painted. The Elizabeth Street number is CONTESTED (SPEC says 251, the photograph reads 267) and must not be painted until read. Nothing else from the machine.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — US$150–400 per board to a working rotulista in Mexico City or Xalapa, plus steel and shipping: under US$2,500 for one windscreen board and four wall boards, which is less than one photoreal render queue costs in electricity. Two to four weeks, mostly the painter's schedule and the post. Named lineage to approach: Imprenta Enlace (Xalapa) for the poster idiom, Alex Kurunis's `Rotulismo Mexicano` for finding painters, Francis Alÿs's commissioning precedent for the ethics. CEILING: those names come from web-search summaries in this session's dossier, NOT primary sources — verify before contacting anyone or printing a credit.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — This week, one A1 sheet of paper and a can of spray mount. Set the itinerary at full size in a heavy condensed sans as a stand-in, print it 900 × 300, mount it to foamboard, and stand it in the windscreen of the bus in the Nolita room. Photograph it from where a customer sits. That photograph IS the pitch, it costs nothing, and it tells you in five seconds whether a route board makes the room better or makes it kitsch.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-2-3`

### EL DE LA CAMISA ROJA  ·  screen 7.5/10

**REGISTER** hand-drawn + polychrome + people-and-food (five measured spot colours, one human hand, one machine hairline)  
**ARTEFACT** 640 × 450 mm landscape screenprint, SIX passes — five spot colours plus one hairline black — on 300 gsm warm uncoated (Colorplan Natural). Edition of 100, numbered, signed by the illustrator. One framed at each site; the rest sold at the counter.

> The six people this project spent seventy-six revisions deleting as measurement error, drawn back in by a human hand, over a bus drawn by the machine.

In `ref_side.jpg` a young man in a red T-shirt leans on the front wheel arch with something white in his mouth. He is eating. The record's only description of him is: "a man stands directly in front of the front wheel — every rev 8 attempt to measure the front arch locked onto his red shirt instead." The cook appears as "37 % of the variance… and no dressing can produce it." A child in Nolita appears as "THREE of its 106 points were ON A CHILD'S HAIR — residuals −16.7, −16.1, −13.6 px." `lid_gen.py` masked "palm fronds, the man at the counter… 6.09 % of the interior." Seventy-six revisions have treated the customers as noise.

This print restores them. The bus is the machine's line — 0.25 pt, true scale, so the illustrator has an exact ruler and every figure is sized against the belt line at 1207 mm above ground rather than guessed. Everything the machine cannot measure — six people, a plate, the jug blender, the palms — is painted by one hand. Every one of the five inks is a colour measured off the vehicle: RED 196/49/36, CREAM 206/208/200, menu-strip yellow 172/144/17, and the two colours the bus's own painted food is painted in — vignette meat PINK 206/118/124, garnish GREEN 96/128/54. Nothing in the palette was chosen.

A 6 pt strip along the foot prints the five rejections verbatim with their revision numbers. That is the joke and the apology in one line of type.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The model is the RULER and the underdrawing, not the picture. A true orthographic side elevation at exact scale is the only way a hand-drawn figure can be sized honestly against the vehicle — belt line 1207 mm AG, arch lip, counter slab — instead of eyeballed off a perspective photograph. The hairline vehicle comes from the line pass; the people come from a brush; the visible join between two authors on one sheet is the concept. This is the one item here where the model contributes scale discipline to a hand drawing rather than making the drawing.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — REAL, IDENTIFIABLE PRIVATE PEOPLE, photographed in a public restaurant around 2010, no releases, on a print offered for sale — including a minor. That is live legal and ethical exposure and I am not sanding it. The only mitigations I accept: the child is drawn from behind, which is how she actually appears in `ref_nolita_front34.jpg` (magenta puffer, back to camera), and she appears ONLY on the restaurants' own wall copies, never on anything sold; the cook and front-of-house are staff and must be asked and paid; the eater cannot be traced, and if the owner will not print a stranger's face the honest fix is to draw him from behind too and say so in the caption — not to invent a different man. SECOND RISK, AESTHETIC: if the illustrator is weak this reads as clip art pasted on a technical drawing. THIRD: sentimentality. This is the piece that can tip into greeting card, and the five cold pixel residuals under a warm picture are the only thing holding it back — DO NOT CUT THE CAPTION STRIP TO MAKE IT PRETTIER. FOURTH, DECLARED NOT SMUGGLED: it composes the VW roundel out by choosing the flank, so F191 stands untouched. FIFTH, F92: this is set at the Playa terrace and F92 rules the Playa hero "DEPRIORITISED, NOT CANCELLED… do not act on it either." Rule 34's object test — F92's object is a PHOTOREAL DELIVERY RENDER through playa_env.py and the studio chain. This is a hand-pulled screenprint using no render, no rig and no playa_env.py, and it is the only artefact in the programme aimed at F92's own phrase, "the emotional bar that sits ABOVE clinical accuracy." I make the defence; I do not re-ask him.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — RED sRGB(196,49,36) and CREAM (206,208,200) read live off `t1_mats.py` this session; STRIP (172,144,17), PINK (206,118,124), GREEN (96,128,54) read live off `lid_gen.py`. Belt line 1207 mm above ground at x=0.962. Body 4065 long, 1750 wide. Roof at rear axle 1983.5 — MODEL reading; the real vehicle's absolute roof height is OPEN AND UNMEASURED. Apertures 515.5 × 403.0, r 55, three, EQUAL. Counter slab 107.0 thick, 321.0 deep, brass nosing 18.6 % of the outer edge, gold h≈32°/s≈0.86. NOT USED AND WHY: INK (72,46,6) is CONSTRUCTED, not measured, so it is not one of the five inks; the counter's height ABOVE GROUND, because source carries it in the un-dropped frame and I have not watched an AG figure print; the pillar asymmetry, retracted.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Illustrator commission US$2,500–6,000 for one plate. Six screens, edition 100 at 640 × 450 on Colorplan: roughly US$14–22 a sheet, US$1,400–2,200 the run. Four to six weeks, nearly all of it the drawing. NO NEW CODE: it wants the line pass, which is the programme's keystone item anyway; until that exists a single 1600×1100 `side` render traced at low opacity is an adequate underdrawing and costs one five-minute render.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — One A3 sheet, this week. Render `side`, print the flank at about 1:8, tape tracing paper over it, and have one illustrator draw ONE figure in colour — the eater: red shirt, cream jacket with maroon trim, dog-tag chain, something white in his mouth — sized off the belt line. Put it in front of the owner with the sentence "every rev 8 attempt to measure the front arch locked onto his red shirt instead" typed underneath. If that one figure does not move him, the other five will not.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-2-1`

### LADO E  ·  screen 5.67/10

**REGISTER** child (colouring mat / restaurant ephemera) — and a survey instrument disguised as one  
**ARTEFACT** A3 placemat, 420 × 297 mm, 80 gsm natural uncoated (crayon takes, no varnish). Litho, TWO colours face (hairline black + menu-strip yellow 172/144/17), ONE colour reverse (black + a 40 % tint of it). Run 5,000, guillotined, no finishing.

> The most accurate drawing of this bus ever made, printed at exactly 1:20, handed to a child with a crayon — and on the back, the side of the vehicle no photograph on earth shows, with a request for one.

FACE: the show side at exactly 1:20 — the machine's line, nothing added, nothing stylised. It comes out 203.3 mm long, 99.2 mm to the roof at the rear axle, 120.0 mm between axles, tyres 33.2 mm across, and the three serving holes at 25.8 × 20.2 mm with a 2.75 mm corner — three of them, equal, because the mesh says equal. A border of the five food vignettes read off the bus's own menu strips: two bolillos, the torta, the tall glass and its orange, the tostada, the prawn. A child colours it in. That is the whole face.

BACK IS THE CONCEPT. SPEC grades the off side of this vehicle E — NEVER PHOTOGRAPHED. Twenty years, four cities, and not one frame of the other flank exists. The reverse prints that side at the same 1:20 as the machine's honest inference, in 40 % tint, under one line: ¿TIENES UNA FOTO DE ESTE LADO? Do you have a photograph of this side? An address to send it to, and a promise — the first usable one gets a name in the corner of the next run.

Thousands of people a month eat off these. Somebody who sat at that table in 2011 has that photograph on a phone. This is the only artefact in the programme that can close a measurement the project has formally declared unrecoverable — "it cannot be recovered from what we hold" — by asking the people who hold it. THE MERCHANDISE DOES THE SURVEY.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Everything, and there is no substitute. No photograph is orthographic, so 203.3 / 99.2 / 120.0 / 87.5 / 33.2 / 25.8 × 20.2 can only be typed because they are the mesh's own figures divided by twenty. And the off side exists ONLY as the model: it has never been photographed, its geometry is an expert inference built into the mesh and graded E, so printing the reverse is literally printing the model's guess and labelling it as one. Neither face of this sheet is producible from the reference set. The reverse also needs no camera work — rotate the model 180° about Z into the existing side ortho, which already fits; the clipping front/rear cameras are not touched.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — ONE: it publishes an admission of ignorance to paying customers on the restaurant's own table. "We measured this bus for seventy-six revisions and we do not know what one side looks like" reads as charm or as incompetence, and only the owner can say which. TWO: the off-side drawing is GRADE-E FICTION shown to the public in a place they will reasonably take as knowledge. The 40 % tint and the question mark are the entire defence and must not be design-tweaked away for looking weak. THREE: SPEC §10.10 is a hard bar — "ABSOLUTE REPLICATION OF ARTWORK", the owner's own words. THIS SHEET OMITS the mural, the paisley, the Señor Tacombi script and the Calidad sunburst, because a 1:20 colouring line cannot carry them and a child cannot colour them. Rule 34's object test: the bar's object is the MODEL and this is a different object — but I DECLARE the omission rather than smuggle it, and that declaration is the price of using it. FOUR: it composes the VW roundel out by being a side elevation. Declared; F191 untouched. FIVE, and this is the one to actually guard: an address on a children's placemat is a data-collection channel aimed at families. It must be a plain email address — no form, no tracking pixel, no list, nothing kept but the photograph.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Body 4065; roof at rear axle 1983.5 (MODEL reading — the real vehicle's height is OPEN AND UNMEASURED, and the small print says so); wheelbase 2400; track 1369/1359; tyre 664.9; width 1750; apertures 515.5 × 403.0, r 55, ×3 EQUAL; rake 17.75 mm/m; ride drop 47.9 mm at x=0; arch gaps 39.7 rear / 40.7 front printed as a PAIR and never as "the front sits higher" (the lock is ±8 mm); hubcap 274.0 with an 86.9 badge; counter slab 107.0 × 321.0; festoon 118 lamps at 28.52 mm as BUILT, with the photograph's ~115 inference flagged beside it; menu-strip yellow 172/144/17; vignette PINK 206/118/124, GREEN 96/128/54, CREAMV 232/224/198. DERIVED BY DIVISION AT 1:20: 203.3 / 99.2 / 120.0 / 87.5 / 33.2 / 25.8 × 20.2 / r 2.75. Small print carries the tail board at BUILT 38.0° against PHOTOGRAPHED 28.0°, different rulers, unresolved seven revisions. NOT USED: the retracted pillar figures; the retired bay taper.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — The cheapest thing in the programme. 5,000 A3 double-sided litho on natural stock: US$350–700, ten working days. Design is one sitting once the line pass exists; before it exists, a `side` render traced at 1:20 is adequate for the proof and for the first run. The reverse costs one extra render at five minutes. No new camera, no fix to the clipping front/rear orthographic cameras, no photoreal chain, so nothing here touches F191 or F193.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — This week: print ONE, on the office printer, both sides, on the brownest paper in the drawer. Put it under his plate at lunch with a box of crayons and say nothing. If he turns it over, the concept works. If he colours it in first and then turns it over, it works better.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-2-2`

## FREE DIRECTOR 3 — the engine is gone

### MANDIL 515  ·  screen 7.17/10

**REGISTER** garment — a designed cut-and-sew garment, not a print carrier; also people and food-and-hand-off  
**ARTEFACT** A cross-back cook's apron in cream cotton drill. The chest panel is a separately cut and seamed rectangle, 515.5 × 403.0 mm, corners radiused 55 mm, faced in body red. ZERO printed inks. Brass hardware, one woven inside-neck label.

> An apron whose chest panel IS a serving hatch at 1:1 — the cook wears the hole they hand the food through.

A cook's apron in cream cotton drill whose chest panel is a serving hatch at 1:1. The panel is cut and seamed as its own piece — 515.5 mm wide, 403.0 mm tall, corners radiused at 55 mm — faced in the body red (196,49,36) on a cream (206,208,200) ground. No printed ink anywhere on the garment. Brass hardware at the strap crossings, taking its colour from the counter's own measured nosing. One woven label inside the neck carrying the rectangle's dimensions and its provenance grade.

The move is that the cook wears the hole. When a plate crosses the counter, the aperture cut in the steel and the rectangle on the chest are the same rectangle — same width, same height, same corner radius — about 300 mm apart. The brand's actual emotional transaction, being handed hot food through a hole by a person standing there, is wearing its own geometry.

And it is the other half of my brief. The vehicle stopped so that a person could stand still inside it. The cook is the only human in this whole record as fixed as the bus, and the project has three times measured AROUND him: a pale band that turned out to be the vendor's shirt, a window that landed on a child's hair, and the flat admission that about 37 % of one bay's variance is the man and no dressing can produce it. This puts him back in, wearing the one number the machine had to fight for.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — One rectangle, and it has to be right. 515.5 × 403.0 at r 55 is not read off a photograph: the bays are ray-tested through the shell — the build prints 'open serving apertures on +Y: 3' — and read back off the mesh at 516 / 515 / 516, THREE, EQUAL. The corner radius is corroborated independently in the photograph at ≈57 mm. The record retired a 0.507 / 0.516 / 0.526 taper TWICE as a perspective artefact, and the pillar asymmetry is forbidden at 0.67σ. The garment is built on a fight the machine won. Beyond that rectangle the model contributes NOTHING, and I will not invent a dependency: the tie height is not a measurement. The counter slab's top sits at z 1254 in the model's UN-DROPPED frame, and its height above ground varies along the vehicle with the 17.75 mm/m rake. I have not watched a single figure print for it and will not put one on a garment. MAKER'S CHOICE — and the label says so.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — (1) The apron is the most-done object on the entire restaurant merch ladder. From three metres this is a cream apron with a red panel and the idea is INVISIBLE until a person is told, once, across a counter. It is a staff-story object; if the staff do not tell the story it is decoration. Say that to him before he orders fifty. (2) The rectangle is FIXED across the size run and the body changes around it. On a small wearer the panel will read oversized and slightly wrong. That is the design and every maker will fight it — the moment anyone scales the panel to the person, the object stops being a measurement and becomes a stripe. Hold the line or do not make it. (3) It carries no wordmark, no Señor Tacombi script and no roundel, so to anyone not told it does not read as Tacombi at all. Declared, not routed around: F191 is untouched and nothing here advances the emblem — 0.8528 against a 0.9465 ceiling, rejected nine times, still shut when this ships. (4) The red is the RED bus's red from `t1_mats`; paint does not transfer between vehicles or livery states, and the white apron in the photograph is what Playa actually wore. This deliberately departs from the photographed garment, and that departure is a design decision, not a measurement.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Serving aperture 515.5 × 403.0 mm, corner radius 55 (photograph corroborates ≈57), THREE, EQUAL, ray-tested; mesh read-back 516 / 515 / 516; body RED (196,49,36) and CREAM (206,208,200) from `t1_mats`; brass nosing linear (0.6600, 0.4750, 0.1750), roughness 0.255, capping 18.6 % of the counter's outer edge; counter slab 107.0 thick, 321.0 deep. Stated on the label as NOT MEASURED: the tie height (counter top z 1254 UN-DROPPED; above ground it varies with the 17.75 mm/m rake and no single figure has been watched print).

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Cut-and-sew: one extra panel, one facing, brass hardware, one woven label. No print set-up, no separations to register, no trademark clearance — zero ink is also zero legal surface, which matters in a programme surrounded by lotería, Talavera and VW marks. A single sample from any maker in New York or Mexico City is a day's work; fifty is an ordinary small order. The only expensive thing is holding the panel dimension across a size run, and that is a spec-sheet instruction, not a cost.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — This week, for the price of a metre of red cotton: take an apron the restaurant already owns, cut a 515.5 × 403.0 rectangle with 55 mm radiused corners, pin it to the bib, and photograph a cook wearing it standing at the counter beside the actual hatch — both rectangles in one frame at the same scale. That single photograph IS the concept, and it is also the first photograph of a person this programme has ever produced.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-3-3`

### TÚ ERES LA TRANSMISIÓN  ·  screen 6.67/10

**REGISTER** packaging as a system — also polychrome and food-and-hand-off  
**ARTEFACT** A takeaway system: natural kraft SOS bag printed 2 inks flexo on the broad face; a 90 mm litho wrap band around each order at 5 inks; a napkin at 1 ink. Bus drawn at 1:20 = 203.3 mm with a true scale bar.

> The engine was scrapped and the transmission sold, so the vehicle cannot leave — which makes the customer the drivetrain, and the bag the only journey it still makes.

A takeaway system, because the vehicle cannot leave and the food can. The bus is drawn once, in side elevation at 1:20 — 203.3 mm from a 4065 mm body — across the broad face of a natural kraft SOS bag, with a REAL scale bar beneath it: one metre, subdivided at 100 mm, printed 50 mm long. Under that, the founder's own sentence and its consequence: 'The engine was scrapped, transmission sold' → MOTOR: NINGUNO. TRANSMISIÓN: VENDIDA. ESTE VIAJE ES TUYO. The customer is the replacement drivetrain. Every bag that leaves the room is the only journey this vehicle now makes.

The second half is the food, and it already exists and has never been drawn at a size a person can see. The vehicle's own menu strips carry five painted vignettes, read off the rectified board at 22×: two bolillos with green sprigs; a torta with pink meat and green garnish; a tall pale glass with an orange; a tostada with magenta filling; a pink prawn. Their palette is MEASURED — PINK (206,118,124), GREEN (96,128,54), CREAMV (232,224,198), on strip yellow (172,144,17). Enlarged to 40 mm and run along the wrap band, they become the brand's food illustration, recovered rather than invented. Tacombi has been carrying its own food drawings on a roof lid for twenty years at 0.05 of a board height, where nobody has ever seen them.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The scale bar is the dependency. Print '1:20' beside a drawing and the drawing has to be TRUE, or the bag is a lie a customer with a ruler can catch. Only the mesh holds 4065 length, 2400 wheelbase, 1369/1359 track, 664.9 tyre OD, 439.6 rim flange, 274.0 hubcap, 39.7/40.7 arch-to-tyre gaps, and the three serving holes EQUAL at 515.5 × 403.0, r 55 — which the record had to defend twice, retiring a 0.507/0.516/0.526 taper as a projection artefact. The Line Art pass also gives the flank's real 5.5 mm shut lines and the V-swage, which a traced photograph flattens. And I will not fake the rest: the five food vignettes need NO model at all. They come off `lid_gen.py` and the rectified board, and the wrap band could be made if the model did not exist.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — (1) The line is a joke about a broken vehicle, printed tens of thousands of times on the object his customers carry home. He may hear MOTOR: NINGUNO as us calling his mark a wreck; and a joke you read twice is worse than none. Do NOT soften it into a slogan — either it lands in ten seconds or the LINE dies and the bag lives. (2) Enlarging the strips is invention past the blur floor. The strip ink (72,46,6) is CONSTRUCTED — the measured median (91,59,7) is blur-limited — and three lines on the side strips are NOT LEGIBLE, resolving only to '?ANO M?' / '??O?A?' / 'Torta?'. SPEC 10.10 makes ABSOLUTE REPLICATION OF ARTWORK a hard bar and I am enlarging artwork past what the record read. The rule-34 defence is stated and not assumed: the bar's object is the MODEL, a bag is a different object. The honest execution is to draw the three illegible lines AS ILLEGIBLE at the size the record read them, and someone will call that precious. (3) Side elevation, so no roundel — declared; F191 untouched and nothing here advances the emblem. (4) Nothing transfers paint from the green vehicle: its priced chalkboard is evidence about the FOOD only, and the vignettes used here are the red bus's own artwork.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Body 4065 → 203.3 mm at 1:20; wheelbase 2400; track 1369 / 1359; tyre OD 664.9; rim flange 439.6; hubcap 274.0; serving apertures 515.5 × 403.0, r 55, THREE, EQUAL; shut line 5.5 mm; arch-to-tyre 39.7 rear / 40.7 front (the 1.0 mm difference is inside a ±8 lock and is NOT printed); PINK (206,118,124); GREEN (96,128,54); CREAMV (232,224,198); STRIP (172,144,17). Founder quote from SPEC §0.1 verbatim. Printed as CONSTRUCTED, not measured: the strip ink (72,46,6).

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Kraft SOS bags at one or two flexo colours are commodity — the bus in line plus red is a two-ink bag with no set-up problems, minimums in the tens of thousands, unit cost in cents. The five-ink vignette row goes on the wrap band and the napkin instead, which are small litho pieces at short runs. Trial: 500 digitally printed bags. No die, no foil, no varnish, no special stock.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — One bag, this week. Print the 1:20 elevation and the scale bar on A3 digital, fold it by hand from a flat-pack SOS template, put a real taco in it and photograph it in his hand at the counter. He is a restaurateur — he will judge it in the two seconds it takes to know whether he wants to be seen carrying it. If the line does not land in those two seconds, we kill the line and keep the bag.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-3-2`

### CUATRO HUELLAS  ·  screen 6.4/10

**REGISTER** child — and hand-drawn / flat colour and line, polychrome at five measured inks  
**ARTEFACT** A kiss-cut vinyl sticker, 135.5 mm long (side elevation at 1:30 from a 4065 mm body), five inks plus the stock, on a 180 × 100 mm backing card printed 1/0. The backing is a second artefact and is the point.

> The die-cut sticker he has been owed since rev 44 — the bus you peel off, and the four marks it has stood on since 6 February 2006, printed at 1:30 on the backing you keep.

A kiss-cut vinyl of the bus in true side elevation at 1:30 — 135.5 mm long, because the body is 4065 mm and the scale is real. Die-cut tight, per his lock: nothing but the bus. The line is the mural's own darkest measured ground (55,26,19); the fills are RED (196,49,36), CREAM (206,208,200), menu-strip yellow (172,144,17) and vignette green (96,128,54). The festoon is drawn with 118 lamps at 28.52 mm pitch because that is what the machine builds — 115 is an inference about a 3.50 m rail, not this model — and they are drawn LIT and warm, which is what both in-service photographs show and what the photoreal render currently cannot do.

The invention is the backing card. One colour, carrying the vehicle's plan at the same 1:30: wheelbase 80.0 mm, track 45.6 front and 45.3 rear, four wheel centres, and the note CONTACT PATCH: UNMEASURED — NO TYRE PRESSURE IS RECORDED. A child peels a bus off four marks and keeps the marks. That is the whole brief inside a child's object: a vehicle designed to move, made to stand, and the act of sticking it down is the act performed on the real one on 6 February 2006.

The height is a POSE and says so in 4 pt: lid angle 76.0° DECLARED, tail board built at 38.0° against a photographed 28.0°, unresolved for seven revisions.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Three contributions, one of them structural. (1) The Grease Pencil Line Art → SVG pass emits a CLOSED PATH, and a closed cut path is the die-line — the identical operation to papel picado, the craft his own locked scene names. The keystone pipeline item and his oldest owed artefact are the same technical problem. (2) 'Cartoon with rendered depth' is his lock and it means occlusion sampled from the asset; only the mesh has an AO pass. (3) `side` is the ONLY orthographic camera in this tree that fits — `studio.py` sets ortho 5.90 against a 4.065 m body while `front` and `rear` are 3.55 and clip — so the one accurate elevation the machine can make is also the one view with no VW roundel in it. The backing card needs true stations: 2400 wheelbase, 1369/1359 track, tyre OD 664.9, rim flange 439.6 (SIXTEEN inch, not fifteen). No die-cast, no LEGO T1, no T-shirt silhouette has ever had those right.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Four, none sanded. (1) His locked scene contains a sun and papel picado and our own SPEC refutes BOTH on the reference — 'there is no sky in frame', the shadows are WARMER, and the top band is 55.1 % foliage, 13.4 % crimson heads, 5.5 % cream florets, no flag silhouettes. He deferred that conflict to himself and must not be re-asked, so we ship the picado as a switch HE flips and print the refutation in 4 pt on the backing. Putting our own doubt on a children's product is the risk; someone will call it cold and they may be right. (2) This fixes a LOOK for this bus for the first time in 76 revisions, and this is the owner who has rejected one drawing nine times. A first look is the thing owners reject hardest — expect a report, not a thank-you. (3) It contains NO food and NO people. Declared, not overlooked: the scene is LOCKED by him at 'nothing but the bus', and my other two concepts carry the hand-off. (4) A side elevation composes the VW roundel out. Declared: F191 is still shut when this ships and this does not touch what it gates. And the standard here is F92's — 'the emotional bar that sits ABOVE clinical accuracy' — borrowed as a STANDARD only. Nothing rebuilds the Playa terrace and nothing touches `playa_env.py`, which is DEPRIORITISED, NOT CANCELLED and is not a work item.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Body 4065 mm; wheelbase 2400; track 1369 / 1359; tyre OD 664.9 (NOT 6.40-15); rim flange 439.6 = 16 in; hubcap 274.0, badge 86.9; bbox top 3.132 m with lids up; roof at tail 1988; festoon 118 lamps at 28.52 mm built (115 is a photograph inference, not this model); RED (196,49,36); CREAM (206,208,200); STRIP (172,144,17); GREEN (96,128,54); GROUND (55,26,19). Printed as UNMEASURED on the face: the contact patch size, the absolute roof height, and the tail board's angle (38.0 built / 28.0 photographed).

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Kiss-cut vinyl, CMYK or 5-spot, 135.5 mm: a few hundred dollars for 1,000 from any local converter, no die charge on a kiss-cut digital run. Backing card is a 1/0 litho or digital piece. The only real cost is the line pass, which is the pipeline item the programme has to build anyway.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Today: run one Line Art SVG side pass off the existing `side` ortho, flat-fill it in the five inks, print it on an office printer, cut it out with scissors, and print the 1:30 backing card underneath it. He can hold the paper version this afternoon and a real kiss-cut vinyl inside a week. If he peels it off the card and the four marks left behind make him smile, we have the deliverable that has been open since rev 44.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — False  ·  **id** `dream:free-3-1`

## FREE DIRECTOR 4 — the contrarian

### MI COMBI  ·  screen 7.4/10

**REGISTER** child · polychrome · packaging (die-cut vinyl) · hand-drawn line over sampled occlusion  
**ARTEFACT** A die-cut vinyl sticker sheet. One hero sticker, 152 × 104 mm, cut to the vehicle's OPEN silhouette at 1:30, five spot colours plus white on white gloss vinyl, kiss-cut on a 165 × 220 mm silicone backing. Two further kiss-cut strips on the same backing: (A) a papel-picado banner, (B) the measured flowering mass. Sheet edge carries one line of 5 pt type.

> The sticker he asked for at rev 44, finally cut — and cut to the outline of a bus that is OPEN, which no VW sticker in the world is.

I am beating PAPEL DE GRASA. That piece is a clever adults-only trick printed on something you throw away with the grease, and its bus is SHUT and dark. Mine is the object the owner has been owed since rev 44, for the audience he actually named — "for children at the restaurant… should spark joy and be something families keep" — and its bus is open for business.

The move: every VW bus sticker ever printed is a closed side elevation. This one is die-cut to the SERVICE silhouette — roof lid up at 76.0°, sign board at 82.0°, tail board propped at 38.0°, rear hatch swung 64.0°, festoon along the drip rail. The cut path spans X_BUMP_F +2.140 m to the tail-board tip at x −2.4133 m and rises to bbox top 3.132 m: 4553 × 3132 mm, drawn at 1:30 = 152 × 104 mm. That shape is spiky, asymmetric, and belongs to one vehicle on earth — the shape of a combi after somebody cut its roof off.

1:30 is a floor, not a taste. Louvre pitch 21.111 mm lands at 0.70 mm; festoon pitch 28.52 mm at 0.95 mm; fringe pitch 26.0 mm at 0.87 mm. Below 1:30 all three go to grey mud. Printed on the sheet edge: DIBUJADO A 1:30 · NO MÁS PEQUEÑO.

Style is his and locked: vector line, flat colour, occlusion sampled from the asset. Wheels drawn the way he said he liked them. Colour is the vehicle's own — red #C43124, cream #CED0C8, gold #EFC245, strip yellow #AC9011, mural ground #371A13.

DECLARED: no VW roundel. A side view never shows it, and after nine rejections that is a choice, not luck.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The open silhouette IS the concept and only the model holds it: LID_OPEN_DEG 76.0, SIGN_OPEN_DEG 82.0, TB_TILT_DEG 38.0, REAR_OPEN_DEG 64.0 and bbox top 3.132 m are one coherent pose no photograph in the reference set contains. The owner's locked style — "shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset" — is literally the Line Art + AO route, so this is the first artefact through the keystone pass. The 1:30 line-weight floor is a model fact too: it falls out of the built pitches (21.111 / 28.52 / 26.0 mm), not out of a printer's spec sheet.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Three, none sanded. (1) It abandons the programme's austere house style outright — five inks, a flat sun that SPEC says is not in the reference frame, and JOY as the acceptance test. The deadpan faithful will call it off-brand; that is the point, and it is the finding the critic wrote. (2) THE PAPEL PICADO. He deferred it himself: "Leave it open, I'll decide when the sticker is actually being built." It is now being built, so HIS OWN TRIGGER HAS FIRED — this is not re-asking a spent question, it is the condition he set (critic's finding 12). The sheet therefore ships with BOTH strips, papel picado and the measured flowering mass (55.1 % foliage, 13.4 % crimson heads, 5.5 % cream florets), and he rules by holding two pieces of vinyl. If he reads that as re-asking, I have spent one of his rulings. (3) An open bus has thin spurs — the tail-board stay, the festoon wire — that will tear off the backing on peel. Bridges fix it and bridges are ugly; if the bridges get big the silhouette loses the only thing that makes it proprietary.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — LID_OPEN_DEG 76.0 (DECLARED, inside brackets intersecting 68.9–78°); SIGN_OPEN_DEG 82.0; TB_TILT_DEG 38.0 (photographed 28.0° on a different ruler — UNRESOLVED, seven revisions); REAR_OPEN_DEG 64.0 (DECLARED, NOT MEASURED, no frame shows it); X_BUMP_F +2.140 m; tail-board tip x −2.4133 m (= −1.853 − 0.7110·cos 38°); bbox top 3.132 m; body L 4065 × W 1750 mm; LOUV pitch 21.111 mm; festoon 118 lamps at 28.52 mm (NOT the inferred 115); FRINGE_PITCH 26.0 mm, FRINGE_R 9.0 mm (INFERRED); hubcap ⌀274.0, badge ⌀86.9 (self-consistency only — never compared to a frame); RED #C43124, CREAM #CED0C8, GOLD #EFC245, STRIP #AC9011, mural GROUND #371A13. UNMEASURED and marked so: the sun (SPEC refutes it — no sky in frame), the papel picado (SPEC refutes it on the vehicle).

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Proof set of ten, digitally printed and contour-cut at any sign shop: $30–60, 72 hours. Production run of 500 at five spots plus kiss-cut: roughly $0.35–0.80 a sheet. No tooling, no minimum order that matters, no new supplier. This is the cheapest object in the entire programme and it is the one with a locked style, a locked scene, a named audience and a written spec — lead with it (critic's finding 10).

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — This week: one A4 sheet on inkjet sticker paper, hand-cut along the open silhouette with a craft knife, under $10. Better within 72 hours: a contour-cut proof of ten. Hand one to a child in the Nolita room and watch what she does with it. That is the whole acceptance test and he can run it himself.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — False  ·  **id** `dream:free-4-1`

### EL TECHO EN LA MESA  ·  screen 7.0/10

**REGISTER** polychrome · beautiful-not-rigorous · food · packaging/product (mercado hule)  
**ARTEFACT** Hule — cotton-backed printed oilcloth, the object every Mexican fonda already owns. Printed on a 1.40 m roll, cut to 1350 × 1350 mm for an 850 mm table top with a 250 mm drop. Seven screens plus one captioned construction. The mural lid reproduced at 1:2 in the field (1017 × 612 mm), the menu strips at 1:2 on two borders, and a 6 pt provenance line along one selvedge.

> The best artwork on this vehicle is painted on its roof where nobody standing at the counter can see it. Put it on the table, under the plates, in all seven of its measured colours.

I am beating PAPEL DE GRASA and NO LEGIBLE at the same time. Papel de grasa has the right instinct — let the food touch the graphic — and then spends it on a disposable sheet in two inks that goes in the bin with the napkin. NO LEGIBLE gives a B0 sheet to everything that could NOT be read. Both compose out the one thing on this bus that IS fully read, fully measured, and genuinely beautiful.

lid_gen.py holds it at 2,569,748 rectified pixels: median (139, 49, 21), saturation 0.835, hue 18.4°, class split 20.26 / 26.19 / 34.06 / 19.49 %. Ten full flower heads plus one half head cut by the right strip. Eight petals a head — k = 8 at power 42.6 against k = 12 at 6.3, which refutes the twelve everyone assumes.

SPEC 10.10 is his own hard bar: ABSOLUTE REPLICATION OF ARTWORK. This is the only concept in the programme whose entire content is obeying it (critic's finding 7). Nothing is restyled. The half head stays half. The five measured food vignettes stay where they are — two bolillos with green sprigs, pink meat and green garnish, a tall pale glass and an orange, a pale shell with magenta filling, a prawn. The three lines at v 0.935–0.966 that are NOT LEGIBLE print as illegible small type, because that is what is true, and that single decision is the whole house style surviving into a warm object.

Selvedge, 6 pt: BOARD ASPECT 1.654, HONEST BAR ±0.09. THREE METHODS GAVE 1.520 / 1.654 / 1.82. INK #482E06 IS CONSTRUCTED; MEASURED MEDIAN (91,59,7). LA SANTA——— IS CONSISTENT, NOT READ.

And it changes what the room looks like on Tuesday.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Very little, and I would rather say that than fake a dependency. The asset is a photogrammetric rectification living in lid_gen.py, not a render — the mural needs the homography, not the mesh. The model makes exactly one real contribution and it is a check, not a source: the build prints the board texture at aspect 1.6621 against lidmural.png's 1.6543, +0.47 %, which is how you know the reproduction on the cloth is not stretched. It also holds the lid opening at 2034 mm so the 1:2 is a true 1:2. That is all. Everything else here was won by a camera and a rectification eight revisions ago and has never been used for anything but a texture map.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Four. (1) AUTHORSHIP, and it is the one that could actually embarrass him. Somebody painted that board by hand and we do not know who. Reproducing it on a product sold in the room is precisely the appropriation this project's own rule-11 discipline should make us allergic to. The only clean answer is Alÿs's: commission a taller — Huixcolotla or a working rotulista — to repaint the seven screens from the rectification, and credit and pay them on the selvedge. That costs money and weeks and it is not optional; if he will not pay it, do not print it. (2) It abandons the austere register completely. This is the loudest object in the programme and the two-ink faithful will hate it on sight. (3) Grease, salsa and a wet cloth will destroy a pigment inkjet in weeks. It must be solvent or screen on cotton-backed vinyl, or it fails in service, publicly, on his own tables. (4) A tablecloth is fit-out, not merchandise. As a metre-good it will be judged against $6 mercado hule, and $6 mercado hule is very good.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Mural rectified over n = 2,569,748 px; median (139, 49, 21); HSV sat median 0.835; hue median 18.4° (IQR 9.9–29.6); class split dark 20.26 / red 26.19 / orange 34.06 / yellow 19.49 %; 10 full flower heads + 1 half cut by the right strip; 8 petals (k = 8 power 42.6 vs k = 12 at 6.3). Inks: GROUND #371A13, GROUND_HI #4A261A, RED #9F2D16, ORANGE #A34215, GOLD #AC7718, PALE #B8964C, STRIP #AC9011. Food vignettes: PINK #CE767C, GREEN #608036, CREAMV #E8E0C6. CONSTRUCTED and captioned as such: INK #482E06 (measured median (91,59,7)) and PEACE #C6AC7A (measured (174,133,73)). Lid opening 2034 mm long, width 1223.7 mm DERIVED; board aspect 1.654 with an honest bar of ±0.09; texture-aspect check 1.6621 vs 1.6543, +0.47 %. Menu-strip word bands v 0.001–0.966; three lines at v 0.935–0.966 NOT LEGIBLE.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — One digital sample, 1350 × 1350 on cotton-backed vinyl: $60–90, five days. Volume: seven-colour rotary screen on 1.40 m hule at roughly $8–14 per metre over 300 m. Twenty tables dressed for about $250. No access to the bus needed, no new geometry, no render queue — the artwork is already on disk and has been for eight revisions.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — Three days, under $50: one A0 print of the 1:2 field on satin paper, laid on ONE table for ONE service, with real plates and real salsa on it. Photograph it at the end of service. He is a restaurateur — he will know inside thirty seconds whether he wants twenty of them, and the grease marks will tell you whether the substrate decision is right before anyone spends on screens.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-4-3`

### AQUÍ TIENE  ·  screen 6.83/10

**REGISTER** three-dimensional · people · food-and-hand-off · environmental  
**ARTEFACT** A 1:1 serving aperture built as a slim backlit case. Cut opening 515.5 × 403.0 mm with r 55 corners, in a 700 × 560 × 75 mm case sprayed cream #CED0C8, carrying a 24 mm bright trim inside the cut edge and a bobble fringe of 65 balls at r 9.0 / pitch 26.0 mm. Duratrans transparency, LED backlight gelled to the measured galley strip colour, linear (1.000, 0.918, 0.790). Hung with its sill at 1307 mm above finished floor. A 200 mm oak step below it.

> The one hole in this vehicle, at full size, lit from inside, with a person on the other side of it holding your food — the brand's actual transaction, made as furniture instead of as a document.

I am beating THE UNCERTAINTY PLATE. That is a drawing, at some scale, on paper, ABOUT how well we know a bus. This is a piece of the bus at 1:1 that your body has to meet. Nobody reads that the sill is 1307 mm; they lean, because it is. Nobody reads that the aperture is 403 mm tall; its head cuts across their view at 1710 mm. A small child cannot see in at all — which is why there is a step, and the step is the measurement made physical rather than dimensioned. (A five-year-old's standing eye height is UNMEASURED here; the 200 mm is sized off the sill, not off her.)

Behind the glass: the cook, and a plate coming through. There is NO human geometry and NO food geometry in this model — no hand, no plate, no taco anywhere in 229 objects, 366,490 faces — so the figure is a photograph and the plate is drawn, and BOTH ARE LABELLED UNMEASURED on the case, in 6 pt, on the same line as everything that is. Everything around them is measured to the millimetre: r 55, the 24 mm trim, the 65-ball fringe, the counter slab 107.0 mm thick and 321.0 mm deep with brass nosing over 18.6 % of its outer edge, and the light itself — one warm strip 20 mm under the head rail, because the record says a serving hatch shows you its strip light.

DECLARED, F92: this is lit from INSIDE. It does not rebuild the Playa terrace, does not touch playa_env.py, and beyond the customer there is nothing. Deprioritised, not cancelled; I am naming it and not acting on it.

DECLARED: no roundel in frame — you are looking at the flank.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Everything that makes it convincing is a model figure a photograph cannot give at this precision: BAY_W 0.5155 × (Z_HEAD − Z_SILL) 0.403 with BAY_R 0.055, the sill and head above ground at 1307 / 1710, FRINGE_R 0.0090 / FRINGE_PITCH 0.0260 / FRINGE_INSET 0.0130, the counter section, and the emission colour and position of the practical light. And the model told me the one thing that shaped the object: the hole is too high for a child, so the piece grew a step. The model also cannot supply the two things at the centre of it, and the piece says so on its own face rather than hiding it.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Four. (1) THE PHOTOGRAPH. The cook in ref_side.jpg — white cap, white shirt, Tacombi apron, hands at the plancha — is a real identifiable person shot around 2006 with no release. Putting him in a commercial fixture is not defensible. Shooting a current cook with a release fixes the law and costs the piece its archive charge; that is a real loss and I am not pretending it isn't. (2) A backlit box in a restaurant is one step from a drive-thru menu board. If the case is even slightly cheap the whole thing collapses into fast food. The case has to be genuinely well made or this must not be built. (3) It reduces 76 revisions of measurement to a hole with a face in it; a purist will say we surveyed a vehicle to build a photo backdrop, and they will have a point. (4) The two things a customer actually looks at are the two things not measured. That is the honest centre of the concept and also its weakest joint — if he hates the drawn plate, there is no measurement to fall back on.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — BAY_W 515.5 × 403.0 mm, BAY_R 55, three of them EQUAL; Z_SILL/Z_HEAD 1307 / 1710 mm above ground; aperture bright trim ~24 mm inside the cut edge; FRINGE 65 balls per aperture, r 9.0 mm, pitch 26.0 mm, inset 13.0 mm (INFERRED, ~2 px each); counter slab 107.0 mm thick, 321.0 mm deep, brass nosing 18.6 % of the outer edge, brass linear (0.6600, 0.4750, 0.1750) rgh 0.255; galley strip emission (1.000, 0.918, 0.790) at GAL_LUM 3.04, 20 mm under the head rail; CREAM #CED0C8. UNMEASURED and printed as such: the cook, the plate, the galley's depth (Y is chosen everywhere, never measured), a child's eye height.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — Slim LED lightbox 700 × 560: $150–400. Duratrans: $60–100. Cream respray or wrap: $80. Fringe and trim, fabricated: $120. Step: $60. Under $700 all in, two to three weeks with one fabricator. It does NOT need access to the physical bus — every dimension comes off the model, which answers the critic's point that every 1:1 idea assumes a bus you can walk up to.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — This week, under $20: print the aperture at 1:1 on paper, cut the r 55 corners with a template, tape it to a wall with the sill at exactly 1307 mm, put a warm lamp behind it. Stand him at it. Then stand a child at it, and watch her fail to see over — that is the moment the concept either lands or dies, and it costs nothing to find out.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — True  ·  **id** `dream:free-4-2`

## FREE DIRECTOR 5 — editions and cultural objects

### EL DÍA QUE LE CORTARON EL TECHO  ·  screen 8.5/10

**REGISTER** child — and with it polychrome and hand-drawn, the three registers the last round had none of  
**ARTEFACT** A picture book. 32pp, 260 × 190 mm landscape, hardback, section-sewn, board covers, uncoated 150 gsm text. Printed 4 colours PLUS one spot for the body red. Fourteen spreads. A die-cut vinyl sticker bound into a pocket in the back board — F18 itself, delivered.

> The true story of a green bus that could not go any further, so it stayed and fed people — for the children the owner has been asking about since rev 44.

A green combi is bought in Mexico City for $3,000, missing three of its windows. Its engine is scrapped. Its transmission is sold. It is towed very slowly through the Yucatán. It stops. Somebody cuts its roof off. The three missing windows become three holes exactly 515.5 × 403.0 mm, and food starts coming out of them. That is the book, and every word of it is in SPEC §0.1 in the founder's own voice.

One spot red because CMYK cannot hold hue 5.0° at saturation 0.816, and this project has already retired one wrong red.

Every spread carries a single small figure in the outer margin, set in the same hand as the pictures. TRES VENTANAS · 515.5 × 403.0 · IGUALES. 118 BOMBILLAS. And on the spread where the bus stands full height against the palms: NADIE SABE QUÉ TAN ALTO ES. Nobody knows how tall it is — H_ROOF was retired at rev 22 and the real vehicle's absolute roof height is OPEN and UNMEASURED. A children's book that prints an unknown, in Spanish, without apologising for it.

The last page is the sticker, in its pocket. Not a giveaway at the till — the end of a book. His own words for what it must be are "something families keep."

This is the artefact that carries what F92 calls "the emotional bar that sits ABOVE clinical accuracy" — quoted here because the critic is right that nobody has quoted it. It is also, at last, the answer to "why do we keep doing revs that don't ship geometry": this ships to a four-year-old.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — The style is not mine, it is his, locked at rev 39: "cartoon with rendered depth — vector line and flat colour, shading and occlusion sampled from the 3D asset." A picture book is fourteen views of one character, and only a model holds a model-sheet across fourteen attitudes while keeping the rake, the three equal holes and the arch gaps honest in every one. Hand-drawn, spread 9 drifts. The roof-cut sequence is literally the lid at 0° and at its built 76°, angles the mesh already carries. And the AO pass is what gives the ink its weight — a uniform traced outline is the giveaway of a fake technical drawing and of a fake picture book alike. This is the one artefact in the program whose style the owner has already specified and which cannot be made without the asset.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — Four, and I am keeping all of them. ONE: it is sentimental, and 76 revisions of this project's culture are anti-sentimental. The first honest objection is that a children's book is where a programme goes when it has run out of rigour. TWO: the true story contains a scrapped engine and a sold transmission, and I am putting a dead motor in a book for four-year-olds. I intend to keep it. That is the moment the vehicle becomes a room, and children take it better than adults do. THREE, and it could stop the print run: the VW roundel appears on the nose in three spreads at roughly 8 mm across. Whether the shipped mark's ~0.09 pose-free IoU deficit against P1b's 0.9465 survives at 8 mm is UNMEASURED. Run that test before plates are made. If the deficit survives, his ninth rejection becomes his tenth, printed two thousand times — and the fallback is to turn those three spreads three-quarter-rear, which is composing the emblem out and must be DECLARED on the colophon, not done quietly. F191's hold is on the photoreal delivery render and does not bind a drawn book; that distinction is stated, not assumed. FOUR: SPEC §10.10's "ABSOLUTE REPLICATION OF ARTWORK" is a hard bar and this book stylises the mural to eight flat colours. Rule 34's object test: the bar's object is the MODEL. The book is a different object. Said out loud, on the page, because the last round did this silently three times. And: the child in ref_nolita_front34.jpg is a real minor. Her likeness is not usable and no character is drawn from her.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — Serving apertures 515.5 × 403.0 mm, r 55, THREE, EQUAL (measured, ray-tested). Wheelbase 2400. Body 4065 × 1750. Roof 1983.5 mm at the rear axle — and H_ROOF RETIRED, absolute roof height OPEN and UNMEASURED, printed on the page as such. Rake 17.75 mm/m. Lid opening 76.0° DECLARED. Tail board built 38.0°, photographed 28.0°, unresolved seven revisions — drawn at 38.0 and captioned as a disagreement or not captioned at all. 118 festoon lamps at a realised 28.52 mm pitch (I watched this print from BULB_X0 −1.6368, BULB_X1 1.7000, BULB_PITCH 0.0286 — the "115" in circulation is an FFT inference about a 3.50 m rail, NOT the built count; the book prints 118). RED sRGB (196,49,36), hue 5.0, sat 0.816. CREAM (206,208,200). Menu-strip yellow (172,144,17). Mural palette GROUND (55,26,19) through PALE (184,150,76). NOT used: the retracted pillar asymmetry; the retired bay taper.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — ESTIMATES, not measured figures. Print is the cheap half: 32pp hardback, 4C + 1 spot PMS, section-sewn, at 2,000 copies roughly US$4–6 landed per unit; the tipped-in sticker adds ~US$0.20 per unit plus a one-off cutting die at ~US$150–300. Order of magnitude US$9–13k for 2,000. The real cost is one illustrator for a quarter. It does NOT block on the Grease Pencil Line Art → SVG pass that the program calls its keystone: spreads can be inked over side-ortho renders by hand while that is built, and the side orthographic camera fits and renders today. If the line pass lands mid-way, the later spreads get better and nobody can tell.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — ONE SPREAD, A3, in his hands this week — and it carries the question the critic says the programme missed. Render the side ortho twice, lid at 0° and lid at 76°. Hand-ink and flat-colour the pair in one session. Print A3. Cut the die-cut sticker on a desktop plotter from the same outline and put it in an envelope taped to the back. Then ONE multiple-choice question, with the crop attached: his scene lock for the sticker is "nothing but the bus, die-cut tight, plus the sun and the papel picado", and SPEC refutes both — the band across the reference is a continuous flowering mass (55.1 % foliage, 13.4 % crimson heads, 5.5 % cream florets) and there is no sky in frame. (A) keep the papel picado, this is a sticker, not the photograph. (B) use the flowering mass that is actually there. (C) bus alone, nothing behind it. He deferred this himself — "Leave it open, I'll decide when the sticker is actually being built." It is now being built. That is the trigger, and it unblocks the oldest live row in the register.

### A MANO ALZADA  ·  screen 6.87/10

**REGISTER** hand-drawn — with people and environmental; the hand that is not a machine trace, which the last round had none of  
**ARTEFACT** A commission and a pair of objects. THE BOARDS: three to five menu boards, industrial enamel on lámina, 1200 × 800 mm, painted freehand by a named working rotulista, each unique, each signed by him. THE SHEET: a folded companion, 2 colours on offset, 500 copies, carrying the overlay and the residual table.

> Pay a real sign painter to paint the bus's menu board freehand on tin, then measure his board against the model and publish the residuals — with the finding framed as being about the model, not about him.

The rotulista is paid a fee, named, credited, keeps a board and keeps the copyright in his own lettering. He is given one thing no sign painter has ever been given for this vehicle: a true orthographic side elevation at 1:10, with the belt line at 1207 mm above ground, the 17.75 mm/m rake, the 5.5 mm shut lines and the V-swage, printed at size. Every photograph of this bus is oblique. Nobody has ever seen it flat.

Then the second half, where this project's actual voice goes. We photograph his board, rectify it onto the same ortho, and publish the residuals. Where his letters sit against the measured script panel, 1278 × 544.3 mm. Where his red sits against (196,49,36), hue 5.0°, sat 0.816. Where his arches sit against 373.5 mm.

He will be wrong, and better. That is the piece.

Francis Alÿs painted small originals, commissioned rotulistas to enlarge them in enamel on tin, then painted again from their copies, collapsing authorship. This inverts it. A machine that has spent 76 revisions auditing itself audits a master, in public, and loses. The trade is called el otro muralismo and it is dying under printed vinyl; this is the answer to the appropriation charge that Alÿs already found — commission the practitioner, do not imitate him.

The sheet is titled so nobody can misread it: NOT HIS ERRORS. OURS.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Two dependencies, both load-bearing. The brief he paints from: `side` is a true orthographic camera and it FITS today — the front and rear ones clip at 2.4406 m against a 3.132 m bbox and are a known fix. Nothing else in the world holds this vehicle flat, because every reference frame is a projection. And the audit: you cannot measure a hand-painted board against a photograph, because a photograph is a projection and a de-squash is not an un-projection (rule 43, learned here the hard way). You can measure it against a mesh, through the homography path this tree already has. The model is simultaneously the brief and the ruler, and it is the only object that can be both.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — FIVE, none sanded. ONE, appropriation, straightforwardly: a US-invested restaurant group buying Mexican vernacular labour for cultural capital. Good intentions do not neutralise it. Terms do, and the terms go on the credit line — fee not wage, his name on the board, his name in every caption, he keeps a board and the copyright in his lettering. If the owner will not agree to those, KILL IT. Do not run it politely. TWO: the residual table is potentially insulting. "His swash is 11 % wide of the model" reads as a machine grading a maestro, and no amount of titling fixes a thing that is actually condescending. The framing has to be genuinely meant. If in the making it starts reading as a scorecard, the table is cut and the boards ship alone — and that is a real possible outcome, not a hedge. THREE: he may simply paint something better than everything else in this programme, at which point 77 concepts look like what they are. I think that is the best thing that could happen and I am aware the programme may not. FOUR: SPEC §10.10's artwork bar. He will re-letter Señor Tacombi in his own hand and depart from the measured silver script. Rule 34 object test — the bar's object is the MODEL; the board is a new object made for the room. Declared here, not silently. FIVE: hanging boards in the Playa room is NOT the Playa hero. F92 is RULED-rev57b "DEPRIORITISED, NOT CANCELLED" and its object is a RENDER; a painted tin board in a physical restaurant does not touch it. Stated so nobody has to wonder. And the VW roundel: a menu board carries no vehicle mark. Composed out by construction, declared, F191 untouched.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — The reference sheet prints: body 4065 mm long × 1750 mm wide (mesh, lids and counter excluded — NOT STATE.md's 4.5800 table row, which includes opened lids; the build's own line says 4.065 and that is what goes on the sheet). Belt line 1207 mm above ground at x = 0.962. Window sill 1307, head 1710 above ground. Rake 17.75 mm/m, 1.02°. Shut lines 5.5 mm. Rear arch radius 373.5 mm; rear arch lip to tyre gap 39.7 mm, front 40.7 — printed as a pair with the ±8 mm lock, NOT as "the front sits higher". Script panel 1278 × 544.3 mm, two independent derivations agreeing to 1.6 mm. Tyre ⌀664.9, rim flange ⌀439.6 — sixteen inch, not fifteen. Señor script silver chromaticity ~(127.4,124.9,130.0), SENOR_MICHELSON 0.1922 ± 0.0060, tarnish SHIPS OFF by his rev-62 ruling "Bright silver, same as Tacombi." The residual table's own ceiling printed beside it: two significant figures on anything read off a photograph.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — ESTIMATES. Rotulista fee US$400–1,200 per board plus enamel and lámina; three to five boards, US$2–7k all in. The fee must be a fee, not a wage, and that is a cost line, not a nicety. The companion sheet: 2-colour offset, folded A2, 500 copies, ~US$400–700. The measurement half is an afternoon and a rectification script on the existing homography path. Order of magnitude US$4–9k. The unknowns are not money and I will not pretend they are: whether a rotulista is reachable, and whether a Tacombi room will hang the boards. Both are the owner's to answer and neither has ever been established — the critic's finding 9(c) in a different coat.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — NOT the board — that is weeks and a painter. The REFERENCE SHEET, this week, one session. Render the side ortho today, plot it A1 at 1:10 with the belt line, the rake, the shut lines and a blank 1200 × 800 menu rectangle laid on the flank where the board would hang. Roll it. Hand it to him. He has never seen this vehicle flat either, and the sheet is the whole concept in one object he can unroll on a table. With it, one question, two options and nothing else: does the first board hang in Nolita or in Playa — and does the painter's name go on the board itself, or only on the sheet? The second half of that question is the ethics of the piece and he should be the one to answer it.

### PELTRE  ·  screen 6.87/10

**REGISTER** three-dimensional — the register the last round had none of at all; secondarily packaging  
**ARTEFACT** An edition of peltre — Mexican enamelled steel. A 9 cm jarro and a 260 mm plate, steel dipped and fired in two colours, the break running round the body. The base carries one fired line: ACABADO: NO MEDIDO.

> The bus is enamelled steel and so is the mug; both chip the same way — so the edition ships perfect, stamped FINISH: NOT MEASURED, and the owner of the mug finishes the measurement the machine could not.

Peltre is the vernacular Mexican kitchen vessel and it is the same object as the vehicle: steel under a vitreous coat, which chips at the edges and shows the dark metal through. The colours come off the shader, not off a JPEG — cream sRGB (206,208,200) over red sRGB (196,49,36), hue 5.0°, saturation 0.816. The two-tone break runs at 0.6085 of the vessel's height, because on the bus the belt line sits at 1207 mm above ground against a roof of 1983.5 mm at the rear axle.

Two things make this an edition and not merchandise.

First: the cream is fractionally GREEN. G 208 against R 206. Nobody has ever printed that, and rev 3 shipped R > G and was wrong. A dipped enamel holds a two-point green shift that CMYK on a menu card cannot.

Second, and this is the whole piece: the vessel ships PERFECT and the base is stamped ACABADO: NO MEDIDO. Finish: not measured. The one thing about this vehicle's surface that the record asked for at rev 53 and has never received is a frame showing the cream where it IS chipped. The band is 0.27 px at every shipped scale. It cannot be recovered from what we hold.

So the chipping is not designed and not faked. Whoever drinks from it makes it, over years, and the object completes a measurement this project could not take. A tolerance has never been printed on a mug.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — Modest, and I would rather say so than manufacture a dependency. The model contributes exactly two colours and one proportion. A competent designer could make this object without it. The reason that is worth stating is what happens when you do it the other way: eyeballing the red off a photograph is precisely what produced this project's hue-26° "orange-red", read off a 246 × 197 thumbnail, contaminated by the gold folk art, shipped, and then RETIRED. The model's contribution here is a NEGATIVE result — it is the only thing in the world that knows the obvious red is the wrong red, and knows the cream is greener than its red channel. That is worth one fired line on the base and nothing more, and nothing more is claimed.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — FOUR, and the first one is real enough to change the product. ONE: this is a health-code object. Chipped vitreous enamel exposes bare steel, and a vessel designed to chip is a vessel that will eventually fail an inspection. I am not going to launder that as "patina". The honest resolution is that the JARRO is the edition piece, sold to take home, and the PLATE does not go into service — and if he wants service ware, the plate is dropped and the concept loses nothing. Anyone who proposes the chipping mechanic on a service plate has not thought about it. TWO: cream-and-red enamelware sits four inches from a homeware-shop cliché. Without the fired base line it is a nice mug and nothing else, so that line is LOAD-BEARING and must be legible and readable — not a tasteful debossed whisper. If it cannot be read, kill the concept, because the mug alone is merch. THREE: peltre is a living Mexican industry with real works. Buying blanks in Asia and calling them peltre is the version of this that deserves to be attacked, and it is the cheap version, so it is the one that will get proposed in month two. Name the works on the base or do not use the word. FOUR, stated plainly rather than argued away: THIS CONCEPT DEPICTS NEITHER FOOD NOR A PERSON. It is the vessel the drink is handed over in — the hand-off as an object rather than as an image. If the completeness critic wants the tray crossing the brass line drawn, concepts 1 and 2 supply it and this one does not. Marked false rather than talked into true. It also carries no VW mark and reproduces no artwork at all, so the emblem is composed out by construction and F191 is untouched — declared, not routed around. Bench adjacency, cited rather than hidden: PÁTINA is a title in the 78; this is a fired vessel whose patina is deliberately NOT made, which is the opposite move.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — CREAM sRGB (206,208,200), MEASURED, and G > R is the point. RED sRGB (196,49,36), linear (0.5520,0.0294,0.0176), hue 5.0°, sat 0.816, MEASURED — with the retired hue-26 orange-red named on the collateral as the error it corrects. Belt line 1207 mm above ground; roof 1983.5 mm at the rear axle; the ratio 0.6085 is DERIVED and labelled DERIVED. Edge-wear Pointiness window 0.520 → 0.600 against a flat flank at 0.500–0.503 — the model knows WHERE a real chip starts even though it cannot see one. And the honest one, fired onto every base: the chip pattern is UNMEASURED, 0.27 px at every shipped scale, and cannot be recovered from what we hold. NOT used: any pillar figure, any bay taper, any "overall height" for the real vehicle.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — ESTIMATES. Peltre is dipped and fired — no die, no plate, tooling near zero, which is why it is the cheapest of my three to reach production. Two colours, 500 units of a 9 cm jarro at roughly US$6–12 landed; the 260 mm plate 2–3× that. Order of magnitude US$4–8k for 500 jarros. The base stamp is a fired or screened mark, cents. THE RISK IS MINIMUMS, not unit cost: several works want 500–1,000 units per colourway, and a two-colour masked break may push the minimum up. Realistic lead time from a Mexican works, 8–14 weeks. Everything in this paragraph is an estimate and none of it is a machine figure.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — ONE MUG, THIS WEEK, about US$20 and an afternoon. Buy a plain white peltre jarro — they are everywhere and they cost four dollars. Mask it at 0.6085 of its height. Spray two automotive enamels matched to (206,208,200) and (196,49,36). Letter ACABADO: NO MEDIDO on the base. Then take a file to the rim and chip it once, deliberately, so he can see what four years will do. Hand him the mug and say nothing about it. If he turns it over, the concept works. If he does not, the base line is not load-bearing enough and the concept is merch — and that is a real test with a real failure state, which is more than a mood board can offer.

**CONTAINS FOOD OR PEOPLE** — False  ·  **id** `dream:free-5-3`

## FREE DIRECTOR 6 — systems and time

### CALENDARIO DE LA CASA — AÑO XXI  ·  screen 8.53/10

**REGISTER** POLYCHROME and PEOPLE — the vernacular Mexican business giveaway calendar in the Galas de México register. Deliberately BEAUTIFUL rather than rigorous, and the only warm thing in the programme.  
**ARTEFACT** Wall calendar. 480 × 680 mm header board, 300 gsm uncoated, SIX units — CMYK plus two measured spots, body red 196/49/36 and menu-strip yellow 172/144/17. Tin strips top and bottom, 12-leaf tear-off block on 80 gsm offset, wire-stitched at the foot. Issued once a year, on 6 February, forever.

> The free wall calendar every Mexican business gives out — one big polychrome painting of the bus in service, handed over on the anniversary and starting in February, because February is when the roof came off.

Above the block, one painting: bay 2 open, an arm coming out with a plate, the woman with her chin on her hand at the formica table waiting for it, orange vinyl chairs, the gingham skirt on the near table only, palms close behind, and the drip rail LIT — 118 lamps at 28.52 mm, warm. That last is not decoration: the photoreal render cannot produce it, because at V 0.95 the view transform takes the chroma out of the emission. The one thing the customer's eye goes to is the one thing the render loses, and a painting gets it for free.

The palette is measured, not chosen: #371A13, #9F2D16, #A34215, #AC7718, #B8964C, #AC9011, #C36E44, #8FAD7F, #AFC7A4, #C3A770, #D0B16F, #463E32 — that is warm dark grey, NOT black — plus the food inks #CE767C, #608036, #E8E0C6. Fifteen colours' worth, all read off frames, in a programme that has been running on two.

It counts in Roman years from 6 February 2006. AÑO XXI runs February 2027 to January 2028 and is handed out on the anniversary, not at New Year, which turns 6 February into an event the house already owns. Years I–XX are printed once, small, on the back, blank: NO EMITIDO. Hang three of these in a row and the series is the point.

**WHAT THE MODEL CONTRIBUTES** — PROPORTION AND A LIGHT KEY, not a picture. The illustrator paints onto a vehicle that is correct — every VW bus in every calendar painting ever made is a caricature with the nose too round — and onto the MEASURED Playa light: 3.95:1 between the flank facing the opening and the tail face 72° away, 12.5:1 across the palm trunk, an up-facing cream surface reading 0.93:1 against a vertical one facing the opening. Hand the illustrator a correct three-quarter line drawing and that light ratio card, and the painting is right before the first brush. The picture itself is made by a person, and should be.

**THE RISK IT CARRIES** — (1) LIKENESS. It draws identifiable real people from private photographs — the cook in the white cap, the woman at the table, the child in the magenta puffer. Without releases that is a real problem, and 'we'll generalise the faces' is precisely the sanding the last round was condemned for. Get releases and keep the documentary truth, or invent the figures and lose it. Both cost something; there is no free version. (2) The Helguera/Galas register is one step from kitsch and one step from pastiching a specific dead artist's manner. The answer is to commission a living Mexican illustrator, which is a budget line and a schedule, not a disclaimer. (3) IT STARTS IN FEBRUARY. A share of customers and staff will call it broken, and some will bin it. (4) F92, NAMED. He ruled the Playa hero DEPRIORITISED, NOT CANCELLED, and the carriers say 'do not act on it either.' This is NOT that: not playa_env.py as a delivery frame, not a photoreal hero, not the render F191/F193 gate — a lithographic painting is a different object under rule 34. But it uses his terrace and it aims straight at the sentence F92 carries and this programme has never once quoted: 'the emotional bar that sits ABOVE clinical accuracy.' HE MUST OPEN IT. Do not build it until he does. (5) It abandons the austerity, and everything else in the programme will look thin beside it. Two house styles need a written rule about which is which, and nobody has written one.

**MEASURED FIGURES USED** — 118 festoon lamps at 28.52 mm realised pitch (computed live from BULB_X1 = 1.7000, BULB_PITCH = 0.0286 — the '115' in circulation is an inference about a 3.50 m rail, not this build); Playa light model 3.95:1 flank-to-tail at 72°, 12.5:1 across the palm trunk, 0.93:1 up-facing cream, foliage at 0.104–0.113× the cream bodywork; body red 196/49/36 (#C43124, hue 5.0, sat 0.816), cream 206/208/200; mural GROUND/RED/ORANGE/GOLD/PALE #371A13 #9F2D16 #A34215 #AC7718 #B8964C; strip yellow 172/144/17; terrace C_VINYL #C36E44, C_GINGHAM #8FAD7F / #AFC7A4, C_LAMINATE #C3A770, C_SIGN_FRAME #D0B16F, C_SIGN_INK #463E32; food inks #CE767C, #608036, #E8E0C6; opened 6 February 2006. CAPTIONED AS CONSTRUCTED, NOT MEASURED: strip ink #482E06 (measured median is 91/59/7 and blur-limited). UNMEASURED: everything about the two women's faces.

**COST / FEASIBILITY** — ESTIMATES, not measurements, and this is the expensive one — say so. Illustration commission for a full polychrome scene from a good living Mexican illustrator: $3,000–8,000. Six-colour litho, tear-off block, tin strips at 5,000 units: ~$1.20–2.00 each. First year roughly $12,000–18,000. And it RECURS every year, which is both the burden and the entire point — a series that only makes sense at number three. Long lead: commission by June to hand out on 6 February.

**FIRST SHIPPABLE STEP** — One A3 print, this week, no illustration spend. Masthead — CALENDARIO DE LA CASA · AÑO XXI · 6 DE FEBRERO DE 2006 — the 12-leaf grid drawn to size, the foot line with the address, and in the image window `ref_rear34.jpg` cropped and knocked back to 40 % with IMAGEN POR ENCARGAR across it. He rules on three things off one sheet: the object's shape, the February start, and the anniversary hand-out. Nothing goes to an illustrator until he does.
